Few things are more comforting than tender pasta tossed in a rich, flavorful sauce, topped with melty cheese, and baked until bubbly and golden. No matter the pasta shape, the type of sauce, or the variety of cheese, there’s just something about a bubbling dish hot from the oven. It’s pure comfort! These baked pasta recipes are some of my favorite go-tos whenever I’m craving a hearty and cozy meal.

Pasta Bakes – The Ideal Dinner Solution

We’ve all been there: dinner is approaching, and you need something everyone will actually eat. Pasta is an easy answer—it’s affordable, quick, and versatile. But after years of cooking and developing recipes, I know that even a family favorite can start to feel repetitive. That’s where these 15 easy pasta bake recipes come in. I use simple pasta as the foundation, then build in hearty proteins like chicken, beef, or seafood, switch up the sauces and flavors, and finish each dish with a generous layer of melty cheese. A quick trip to the oven transforms those everyday ingredients into bubbling, hearty meals that make dinner feel anything but boring. Here are 15 of my favorite pasta bakes to add to your dinner rotation.

Tips for Making the Best Pasta Bake

  • Don’t overcook the pasta. Prepare the pasta al dente, as it will continue to cook while it bakes with the other ingredients. Overcooked pasta will get soggy and potentially fall apart once boiled and then baked in the oven.
  • Broil for a few minutes. If you like browned, bubbly cheese, broil for just the last few minutes.
  • Good sauce coverage. One thing you want to do is make sure the pasta is covered in sauce; otherwise, it will dry out as it bakes.
  • Rest before serving. All these recipes are loaded with sauce and cheese, so if you serve them straight from the oven, they’ll fall apart. Give the casserole a few minutes to firm up once you take it out of the oven.

The annual celebration returns October 31 through November 2 at all eight Valley locations.

(RestaurantNews.com)  Día de los Muertos is a time to celebrate life, honor loved ones, and gather over the traditions that bring people together. From Saturday, October 31 through Monday, November 2, Barrio Queen invites the Valley to celebrate with authentic Mexican cuisine, festive décor, custom altar displays, and special drink offerings at all eight locations.

Each Barrio Queen will feature its own unique altar display, giving guests a different experience at every location while honoring one of Mexico’s most meaningful traditions. Guests can gather with family and friends, enjoy traditional Mexican favorites, and raise a glass to the occasion.

Authentic Mexican Food and Drink Specials

Great food is at the heart of every celebration. Guests can pair Barrio Queen’s authentic Mexican dishes, from tacos to flavorful entrées, with handcrafted margaritas and refreshing Mexican beers throughout the three day celebration.

Featured drink specials, October 31 through November 2:

  • $4 Modelo Especial and Modelo Negra
  • $5 Barrio Margaritas, frozen or on the rocks. Add $1 for frozen mango, strawberry, or peach.
  • $7 Skinny Cholas
  • $7 La Flecha Negra

Whether gathering for dinner, sharing a meal with friends, or enjoying a favorite margarita with tacos, Barrio Queen welcomes everyone to celebrate Día de los Muertos the Barrio Queen way.

The Truth About ‘Natural Flavors’ On Food Labels Might Surprise You

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

The word “natural” comes with a few caveats.

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Sarma is a traditional Balkan dish of seasoned minced meat and rice wrapped inside pickled or fermented cabbage leaves, then slow-cooked for hours. Writer-at-Large Aleks Zecevic‘s mother starts pickling the vegetables back in October in anticipation of the family’s Orthodox Christmas celebration in early January. She ferments whole heads of cabbage, alongside cucumbers, cauliflower, carrots, and green tomatoes, saving the finest leaves for her sarma. They’re wrapped around pork, beef, bacon, and rice, then simmered with smoked pork bones until their flavors become inseparable.

Serve alongside a textured, savory skin-contact wine. Its aromatic lift rises above the smoke and richness, while its grip holds firm against the fermented cabbage (see our pairing suggestion below).

“This is my mother’s recipe, although calling it a recipe seems inadequate,” says Zecevic. “It is closer to an inheritance measured in handfuls.”

Sarma

Adapted from Aleks Zecevic’s mother

Ingredients

  • 1 large head fermented sour cabbage
  • ²⁄₃ pound ground pork
  • ²⁄₃ pound ground beef
  • ¼ pound bacon, finely chopped
  • ½ cup uncooked white rice, rinsed
  • 1 large onion, finely chopped
  • 2 garlic cloves, minced
  • 2 bay leaves
  • 1 to 1½ pounds smoked pork bones or ribs
  • 1 tablespoon lard or neutral oil
  • Water or light stock, as needed
  • Salt and freshly ground black pepper, to taste

Directions

Separate the cabbage leaves and trim away the thick central ribs. If the cabbage is especially salty or sour, rinse the leaves briefly in cold water.

Cook the onion in the lard over medium heat until softened. Add the garlic, followed by the rice, and cook for only a few minutes. The rice must remain undercooked because it will finish cooking inside the rolls. Allow the mixture to cool slightly.

Combine the pork, beef, bacon, onion mixture, paprika, salt, and pepper. Place a small amount of filling near the base of each cabbage leaf. Fold in the sides and roll firmly, leaving enough room for the rice to expand as it cooks.

Line the bottom of a heavy pot with leftover cabbage leaves. Arrange the sarma snugly in layers, placing smoked pork bones, bay leaves, and scraps of cabbage between them. Add enough water or stock to come nearly to the top.

Cover and cook very gently on the stovetop, or in a 325°F oven, for approximately 3 hours. Check occasionally and add more liquid if necessary. Remove from oven and serve. Sarma is even better reheated the following day, once the meat and fermented cabbage have had time to meld. Serves 6-8.

Wine Pairing

“Baša Tamjanika 2019 brings floral and tea-like aromatics that brighten the dish, while its texture and grip stand up to the smoked meat and fermented cabbage,” Zecevic says. “I’d also suggest any aromatic, skin-fermented white, or an acidic red, like Mondeuse or Blaufränkisch.”

This article originally appeared in the December 2026 Family Issue of Wine Enthusiast magazine. Click here to subscribe today!

I Tried Hungryroot for 4 Weeks in a Row — Here’s My Unfiltered Review as a Food Editor

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →
Find out if it’s worth it. READ MORE...
📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

If you’re looking for a filling, comforting dinner that hits all the right marks without leaving you with a sink full of dishes, this Creamy Chicken and Tortellini Skillet is about to become your new weeknight obsession! Tender, seasoned chicken, plump cheese tortellini, sweet onions, and aromatic garlic come together in a velvety Parmesan cream sauce. Packed with lean protein and a healthy dose of spinach, it’s a complete, satisfying meal that goes from skillet to table in under 30 minutes. My husband even asked me to make an extra batch so he could bring some to his buddies at work!

From the Freezer to Creamy Comfort Food

The real beauty of this creamy chicken tortellini is its one-skillet convenience and how it delivers maximum flavor with hardly any effort (minimal cleanup? Count me in!). I season the chicken before coating it in a little flour, which helps thicken the broth and pan juices into a creamy sauce without making a separate roux. Even the frozen tortellini goes straight into the skillet without precooking. I add the cream when the broth is a little cooler to keep the sauce smooth, and I leave it slightly loose because the tortellini keeps soaking it up as it sits (which makes it even better for leftovers!).

Frozen spinach is another budget-friendly shortcut I love here. It goes straight from the freezer into the skillet with no chopping, making this a restaurant-worthy dinner without the expensive ingredients or extra prep! Prefer a meat-free alternative? Just leave the chicken out of this dish and swap the chicken broth, or try our spinach tortellini skillet next.

Recipe Success Tips

  1. Butter and olive oil are better together here! I use both because the olive oil helps coat the skillet for sautéing, while the butter adds a rich flavor that carries through into the creamy sauce. Keep the heat at medium, though, as butter can still burn when mixed with oil.
  2. Don’t cut the chicken too small! Aim for evenly sized, generous bite-sized pieces (check out my step-by-step photos below for reference). Chicken shrinks as it cooks, and tiny pieces can dry out quickly. Giving the chicken some room to make contact with the hot skillet also helps develop a little golden color and extra flavor. Just make sure the chicken reaches 165°F internally before serving.
  3. Keep the tortellini FROZEN until you’re ready to use it. No thawing or boiling beforehand! This recipe is designed for tortellini straight from the freezer, so thawing it first can throw off the cooking time and leave you with mushy pasta. Different brands may cook a little faster or slower, so check that the tortellini is tender all the way through before serving.
  4. A gentle simmer is your friend. Once the heavy cream and Parmesan go in, I keep the heat at medium-low. You only want small and gentle bubbles. Too much heat can reduce the sauce too quickly or cause the cheese to turn grainy instead of melting smoothly. Stir everything gently, too, so those little cheese-filled tortellini stay intact!
  5. Customize your skillet! I think adding peas or substituting them instead of spinach would be delicious. Asparagus or broccoli florets would also be delicious choices for substitutes or additions.

Creamy Chicken and Tortellini Skillet

This Creamy Chicken and Tortellini Skillet cooks everything together in a garlicky Parmesan sauce with spinach. No need to boil the pasta separately! Course Main Course Cuisine American Total Cost $13.94 recipe / $2.32 serving Prep Time 10 minutes minutes Cook Time 20 minutes minutes Total Time 30 minutes minutes Servings 6 servings (1.5 cups each) Calories 595kcal Author Jess Rice

Equipment

  • Large Sauté Pan

Ingredients

  • 1 lb. chicken breast I used just over that- 1.31lbs, $6.31*
  • 1 small yellow onion diced, (5.79 oz) $0.42
  • 3 garlic cloves minced, $0.18
  • ½ tsp salt $0.01
  • ½ tsp black pepper freshly cracked, $0.03
  • ½ tsp garlic powder $0.03
  • ½ tsp Italian seasoning $0.04
  • ¼ cup all-purpose flour $0.05
  • 1 Tbsp olive oil $0.09
  • 2 Tbsp salted butter $0.20
  • 2 cups chicken broth $0.26**
  • 19 oz. frozen cheese tortellini not thawed, $3.75***
  • ¾ cup heavy cream room temp, $1.11
  • 1 cup frozen spinach $0.32
  • ½ cup Parmesan cheese $1.14
US Customary – Metric

Instructions

  • Gather ingredients.
  • Dice chicken breast into bite-size pieces. They will shrink up a little as they cook.
  • Mince onion and garlic. Set aside for now.
  • Add salt, black pepper, garlic powder, and Italian seasoning to the diced chicken. Toss so every piece of chicken is seasoned.
  • Then, add the flour to the seasoned chicken. Toss until fully coated on all sides.
  • In a large sauté pan, add olive oil and salted butter over medium heat.
  • Once the butter has melted, add the diced onion. Cook the onions until glossy and softened, about 4 minutes.
  • Nudge the onions to the edge of the sauté pan and pour the chicken into the pan, giving each piece of chicken some surface contact with the hot pan.
  • After 5 minutes, flip the chicken and sear on the opposite side, cooking for an additional 5 minutes.
  • Add the minced garlic and deglaze the pan with the chicken broth, stirring the onions and the chicken together. Let that simmer on medium-low for 4 minutes.
  • Add the entire package of frozen cheese tortellini to the simmering chicken and broth mixture. Stir the frozen tortellini until well-combined.
  • Then, add the heavy cream. The frozen tortellini has lowered the temperature of the broth mixture which will lessen the chances of a curdled sauce.
  • Stir everything together and add the shredded Parmesan and frozen spinach. Stir to combine and simmer over medium-low heat for 2 more minutes.
  • The sauce will remain loose but will thicken slightly due to the starch from the cooked tortellini and the shredded cheese. I like the sauce to remain a little loose, because the tortellini will continue to soak up the sauce as it sits. (And I find it’s better for leftovers!) It should lightly coat the back of a spoon.
  • Taste and adjust the salt and pepper to your liking. This is delicious hot, warm, or cold!

See how we calculate recipe costs here.

Notes

*Boneless, skinless chicken thighs should work well here too. For food safety, check that the chicken reaches an internal temperature of 165°F (74°C), measuring the thickest pieces. **To keep costs low, we use Better Than Bouillon to make our broth. Making it from scratch will help you control the sodium in your recipe, but I recommend tasting the dish before adding additional salt, as bouillon does tend to be on the salty side. ***Keep your cheese tortellini frozen until the step where they hit the pan. If they are defrosted, they will likely overcook and become mushy. The amount of time required to cook them in this recipe from a frozen state results in perfectly cooked tortellini.

Nutrition

Serving: 1.5cups | Calories: 595kcal | Carbohydrates: 48g | Protein: 35g | Fat: 29g | Sodium: 1166mg | Fiber: 5g

how to make Creamy Chicken and Tortellini Skillet step-by-step photos

Gather and prep your fresh ingredients. Keep the frozen tortellini in the freezer until you’re ready to add it.

Prep the chicken: Dice 1 lb. chicken breast into even, bite-sized pieces, keeping them slightly larger than you might think since they’ll shrink as they cook.

Prep the aromatics: Now dice 1 small yellow onion and mince 3 cloves of garlic.

Season and coat the chicken: Add ½ tsp salt, ½ tsp black pepper, ½ tsp garlic powder, and ½ tsp Italian seasoning to the diced chicken. Toss until every piece is evenly seasoned.

Then sprinkle over ¼ cup all-purpose flour.

Toss again until the chicken is coated on all sides with the flour.

Sauté the onion: Heat 1 Tbsp olive oil and 2 Tbsp salted butter in a large sauté pan over medium heat.

Once the butter has melted and begins to gently bubble, add the diced onion. Cook for about 4 minutes, stirring occasionally, until the onion looks glossy, softened, and slightly translucent.

Sear the chicken: Push the onions to the edges of the pan, then add the flour-coated chicken, spreading the pieces out so they contact the hot surface. Cook for about 5 minutes, until the undersides are lightly golden, then flip and cook for another 5 minutes.

Avoid moving the chicken around too much while it sears. Let it sit against the hot pan to develop color and flavor.

Add the broth and garlic: Pour 2 cups chicken broth into the skillet along with the 3 cloves of minced garlic, stirring to combine everything with the chicken and onions. Gently scrape the bottom of the pan to release any flavorful browned bits. Reduce the heat to medium-low and let everything simmer gently for about 4 minutes. The broth should start to look slightly thickened as the flour coating incorporates into the liquid.

Add the frozen tortellini: Pour the entire 19 oz. package of frozen cheese tortellini directly into the simmering chicken and broth mixture. There’s no need to thaw or precook it. Gently stir to distribute the tortellini throughout the skillet without breaking the delicate pasta.

Make it creamy: Pour in ¾ cup room-temperature heavy cream and stir gently to combine. Adding the cream after the frozen tortellini helps because the pasta has slightly cooled the broth, reducing the chance of the sauce curdling. Keep the heat at medium-low so the sauce simmers gently rather than boiling vigorously.

Add the cheese and spinach: Stir in ½ cup shredded Parmesan cheese and 1 cup frozen spinach. Continue simmering gently over medium-low heat for about 2 minutes, stirring occasionally, until the cheese has melted into the sauce and the spinach is heated through. The sauce should look smooth and creamy, with the spinach evenly distributed. Check that the tortellini is tender all the way through, simmering for longer if needed. Make sure the chicken has reached an internal temperature of 165°F.

Check the sauce and serve: The sauce should remain slightly loose but still lightly coat the back of a spoon. Don’t worry if it looks a little thinner than a typical Alfredo sauce! The tortellini will continue absorbing the liquid as it sits, thickening the sauce naturally.

Taste and adjust the salt and pepper to your liking. Serve and enjoy!

What to Serve with Creamy Chicken Tortellini

This recipe makes 6 generous servings of about 1½ cups each. It’s perfect for dinner tonight, with leftovers to look forward to at lunchtime tomorrow! I would pair this with a bright, fresh salad with a decent vinegary bite to cut through the richness of the oh-so-creamy sauce and cheesy tortellini. My go-to is our Greek salad, but this kale salad is another one of my absolute favorites! The subtle sweetness from the raisins and green apples really balances out this creamy chicken skillet.

For something warm, our air fryer Brussels sprouts have deliciously crispy edges and are ready in about 20 minutes, so you can whip them up while the tortellini cooks. Or if you love a little bread with your saucy pasta, our no-knead focaccia rolls can be made ahead of time, making dinner even easier.

Storage & Reheating

Let any leftovers cool, then transfer them to an airtight container and refrigerate within two hours of cooking. They’ll keep for up to 3-4 days. The tortellini will soak up the creamy sauce as it sits (which is why I especially like the sauce to be looser when I know I’ll have leftovers!), so expect it to thicken in the fridge.

Reheating

Warm leftovers in a skillet over medium-low heat. You may need to add a splash of chicken broth or milk to loosen the sauce if the pasta has absorbed a lot. Stir gently until heated through to 165°F. You can also microwave individual portions in short intervals, stirring between each one for even heating.

Freezing

I wouldn’t recommend freezing this one. The cheese-filled tortellini can lose its tender texture, and the cream sauce may separate once thawed. You’ll get much better results keeping any leftovers in the fridge and enjoying them over the next few days.

Pumpkin Top Cocktail

The bold, sweet depth of slow-aged black garlic debuts November 5 in two BBQ entrees, along with a new Pumpkin Top Cocktail just in time for the holidays

Signal Hill, CA  (RestaurantNews.com)  Lucille’s Smokehouse Bar-B-Que, the award-winning destination for authentic Southern bar-b-que that has welcomed guests with slow-smoked meats and down-home hospitality since 1999, today announced the November 5 launch of Black Gold, its first new signature bar-b-que sauce in over five years. Building on 25 years of bar-b-que craftsmanship, Black Gold brings black garlic, slow-aged until dark and sweet as molasses, to two brand-new, celebratory entrees:

Black Garlic BBQ Baby Back Ribs: Lucille’s signature tender, slow-smoked baby back ribs brushed and glazed with rich, dark Black Gold BBQ sauce.

Black Garlic BBQ Baby Back Ribs

BBQ Tri Tip with Black Garlic Butter: Tender, certified Angus Beef tri tip smoked to perfection and crowned with a decadent Black Garlic Butter Sauce.

BBQ Tri Tip with Black Garlic Butter

Also launching on November 5 is a new Pumpkin Top Cocktail with Ketel One Vodka, Owen’s Espresso Martini Mix, and vanilla syrup served on the rocks with housemade pumpkin cold foam.

Both are coming to the menu just in time for the holiday gathering season and will be available at all 19 Lucille’s locations across California, Nevada and Arizona.

Lucille’s has built a reputation on the hallmark of great Southern-inspired bar-b-que: expertly seasoned meats, patient, low-and-slow smoking, scratch made favorites, and genuine Southern hospitality. That dedication has the earned the brand repeat reader-voted honors for its Southern specialties over the years. Black Gold marks the latest step in Lucille’s evolution, introducing a distinctive new flavor while honoring the smoky richness and comforting, soulful food that define the brand.

“Black garlic is having a moment, and we saw an opportunity to bring this bold flavor to the comforting bar-b-que our guests know and love,” said Joan Hansen, vice president of marketing for Lucille’s Smokehouse Bar-B-Que. “Black Gold is a meaningful addition to our sauce lineup, balancing deep savory notes with a natural sweetness that pairs beautifully with our slow-smoked meats. We’re excited to introduce this new flavor and give our guests something special to enjoy as we head into the holiday season.”

For more information or to find your nearest Lucille’s location, visit LucillesBBQ.com.

Third Utah restaurant continues the brand’s growth, opening in 2027

Sandy, UT  (RestaurantNews.com)  Rodizio Grill, America’s First Brazilian Steakhouse, today announced plans to open its third Utah restaurant in Ogden at 2309 Washington Blvd. in 2027. The new location will bring Rodizio Grill’s signature churrasco experience to Northern Utah – rotisserie-grilled meats carved tableside by Rodizio Gauchos, a fresh gourmet salad bar, a Brazilian hot side bar and the warm, welcoming hospitality for which Rodizio is known.

“In 1996, we opened the first Brazilian steakhouse in the United States. When we came to Utah three years later, we were introducing many guests to Brazilian churrasco for the very first time,” said Ivan Utrera, founder of Rodizio Grill. “Utah welcomed us like family, and we have never taken that for granted. Ogden gives us the chance to share our heritage with a new community – the food, the music and the alegria of a Brazilian experience. I couldn’t be prouder to see this next chapter begin.”

“Ogden will be our third Utah restaurant, with a fourth already in the works, and it reflects the momentum behind our brand,” said Steven Staples, CEO of Rodizio Grill. “Utah is home for Rodizio Grill. Our headquarters is here, and Utah guests have embraced us for 27 years. Ogden’s strong sense of community makes it a natural next step. We’re focused on thoughtful growth, bringing the Rodizio experience to communities that are ready to celebrate with us.”

Leading up to opening day, Rodizio Grill will share its “Road to Rodizio” countdown with the Ogden community, including sneak peeks, behind-the-scenes moments and grand opening celebrations. Guests can join Club Rodizio at Rodizio.com to receive opening updates, invitations and exclusive offers – plus a free birthday meal once the Ogden location opens.

More details on the grand opening will be announced in the coming months.

Rodizio Grill’s all-inclusive menu offers unlimited Brazilian sides, more than 30 gourmet salads, and rotisserie-grilled meats and grilled items carved tableside by Rodizio Gauchos. 📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Dim sum translates, with poetic license to “touch the heart.” Indeed, a brunch-hour parade of Cantonese comfort snacks will make hearts young and old become “a flailing fawn,” the expression for hearts going pitter-patter in the dim sum heartland of Guangzhou. In the U.S., dumplings and buns tend to dominate, but they’re only part of the appeal; turnip cake, bone-in spareribs, and gelatinous chicken feet bring a range of textures and tastes that can further liven up the table.

Though tea is the typical pairing, wine has a way of enhancing the social aspect of dim sum while adding layers of flavor to the sprawling spread. If the restaurant allows, bring a few bottles to find your own mix-and-match favorites.

Har Gow (Shrimp)

The best har gow dumplings are stuffed with shrimp and only lightly seasoned to bring out the shrimp’s intrinsic sweetness. “Shellfish wines” like Muscadet, Albariño, and Chablis can work here, but try Carricante from Sicily. Its high acidity, notes of citrus zest and blossom, and a flinty or sea-spray minerality are a slam dunk with all kinds of seafood-based dim sum (like shrimp siu mai, scallop dumplings, and crab-filled soup dumplings).

Tornatore 2024 Carricante (Etna)

An herbaceous, floral nose unfurls aromas of lemongrass, pine needles, wild thyme and honeysuckle. The lemon expands on the palate, before the herbs and forest floor elements return to create a full, round sensation. 93 Points — Danielle Callegari

Inquire with the importer

Char Siu Bao (Pork)

These baked or steamed buns with BBQ pork in an unctuously sweet and savory sauce are often paired with pu-erh, a fermented and aged dark black tea. For wine, Mencía from Galicia is lighter and fresher than the more common Mencía from Bierzo, an intriguing combination of fine tannins, tart red fruit, and stony minerality on a lightweight, almost ethereal, frame. Like the char siu filling, it hints at sweet and savory with a touch of star anise. 

Alvaredos-Hobbs 2022 Mencía (Ribeira Sacra)

Inviting aromas of strawberry-rhubarb, dark chocolate and sagebrush open on the nose along with blackberry jam and hibiscus. The supple palate acknowledges svelte tannins that swoon on the finish like a boy band finishing their final ballad. Decant and drink now, or enjoy from 2027 onwards. Cellar Selection. 94 Points — Reggie Solomon

$40 Vine Republic

Potstickers (Vegetable)

Stuffed with greens, cabbage, mushrooms, and/or chives, crisp-bottomed veggie potstickers can be even more complex than their meaty counterparts, with underlying notes of ginger, garlic, and Shaoxing wine. All these pungent notes can be tricky to pair with wine, but dry Furmint brings its own complementary notes of citrus, ginger, smoke, and green chile, with light body and brisk, cleansing acidity. It has personality without obscuring the dumplings themselves.

Tempos Vega Sicilia 2021 Oremus Mandolás Dry Furmint (Tokaji)

White peach and cantaloupe flavors complement the tart lemon juice on the palate of this nuanced dry Furmint, which also has delicate floral and complex minerally notes. The finish is long and will have you reaching for another sip immediately if not sooner. 92 Points — Emily Saladino

$38 Wine.com

Rice Rolls (Beef)

A textural wonder, these slick, slurpy rolls are filled with chewy, richly seasoned beef; the two elements seem to call for opposing wines. Grignolino, a Piemontese grape with a growing presence in California, produces very light wines with surprisingly firm tannins. It can show flavors that play well with Chinese food: cherry, hibiscus, goji berry, roses, white pepper, and clove. It’s a good red option for all sorts of Cantonese dishes.

Braida di Giacomo Bologna 2023 Limonte (Grignolino d’Asti)

Grignolio, it is in the name, you cannot help but grin from this wine. Opening with aromas of drying raspberries, strawberries and cranberries that merge with notes of sage, thyme, and dusty earth on the joyful nose. Crunchy red berries pop on the palate, with blood orange and citrus notes adding a zestiness to the palate. The finish brings a savory twist of rosemary and mint, all elevated by vibrant acidity. A wine that also likes a chill. 93 Points — Jeff Porter

$28 K&L Wine Merchants

If you’re going with just one bottle, try a dry Alsatian Riesling. It has the body and ripe-fruit expression to pair with delicate cabbage or shrimp dumplings and sweet-and-smoky pork and beef preparations, and it harmonizes with salty, vinegary, and spicy condiments. And of course, Riesling’s trademark acidity cleanses the palate not just from fattier meats, but from the sheer quantity of food that is irresistible at a dim sum feast.

Domaine Paul Blanck 2019 Grand Cru Schlossberg Riesling Riesling (Alsace)

Full of flavor and confidently expressive, with guava and mangosteen lending an exotic edge to a core of yellow apple. The fruit is generous yet clearly defined, kept in check by bright, well-judged acidity that brings energy and structure. The finish is crunchy and precise, adding textural allure and extending the wine’s vibrant, layered character. Cellar Selection. 93 Points — Aleks Zecevic

$42 Saratoga Wine

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

It’s officially soup season, and I’m SO ready for it! This easy Ham and Bean Soup is loaded with tender canned white beans, colorful veggies, and savory chunks of browned ham, all in a thick, creamy broth (made without any actual cream or dairy). It’s a great way to use up leftover holiday ham, but I certainly don’t wait for a holiday to make it! I use just 8 basic ingredients plus a few seasonings to make this hearty, easy, budget-friendly soup, and it’s ready in less than an hour!

“This is excellent. I added leftover chopped up asparagus and four cups of the chicken broth. If ham is not an option smoked kielbasa is great too. I topped the soup with mozzarella an smoked parmigiana cheese.”

Julie W

How I Build Big Flavor in Less Time

Traditional ham and bean soups often start with dried beans and a ham bone that simmer together for hours. And there’s definitely a time and place for that! But I wanted an easy version that didn’t require soaking beans or spending all afternoon waiting for dinner. So I use canned cannellini beans and fully cooked ham to get a head start. They’re already cooked, which means I can spend my time building flavor instead.

One thing I do differently is brown the ham before adding anything else. This gives the edges a little color and leaves behind tasty browned bits on the bottom of the pot (called fond). When I add the onion, carrots, celery, and garlic, their natural moisture loosens those bits and brings that extra flavor into the soup. Sautéing the vegetables also softens them and brings out a little natural sweetness. For the broth, I borrowed a trick from my white bean soup and purée one can of beans with its liquid. This thickens the soup and adds a lovely creaminess without flour, cornstarch, or heavy cream. I leave the other two cans whole for a chunky texture, and after the initial flavor-building stages, everything comes together with just 15 minutes of simmering!

Recipe Success Tips

  1. Give the ham a chance to brown. I cook the diced ham in oil over medium heat for 3-5 minutes, until the edges are lightly browned and golden-brown bits form on the bottom of the pot. This builds the salty ham flavor you’d normally get from simmering a ham bone for hours, just in much less time! If those bits start turning very dark, lower the heat so they don’t burn.
  2. Blend one can of beans, liquid and all! Purée ONE can of cannellini beans with its canning liquid, then drain the other two. The smooth beans thicken the broth, and the whole beans give the soup its chunky texture. No blender? You can thoroughly mash that first can with a potato masher or fork instead. It won’t be as smooth, but this is a chunky soup anyway!
  3. Let the soup boil and thicken. The 15-minute boil allows some of the broth to evaporate while the puréed beans thicken the soup. I give the pot a good stir every few minutes, making sure to scrape along the bottom, to keep the beans from sticking. If it gets thicker than you’d like, stir in a little more broth or water until you reach your preferred consistency.
  4. Taste before adding salt. Cooked ham is pretty salty, especially when it’s cured, and chicken broth adds even more seasoning. That’s why I wait until the ham goes back into the pot before tasting and adjusting. I didn’t need any extra salt in my batch, but I finished each bowl with a little extra black pepper.

What Kind of Ham Can I Use?

For this quick ham and bean soup, I recommend fully cooked ham (or leftover cooked ham from another meal) rather than a ham hock. We’re using canned beans and other ingredients that cook quickly, so there’s no need to spend hours simmering a ham bone to flavor the broth! Thicker pieces work best because they hold their shape and develop nicely browned edges when sautéed.

Boneless vs. Bone-In Ham Steaks

Both of these ham steaks are fully cooked and work well in this recipe:

  • Boneless ham steak: My easiest choice! There’s no bone to work around, and the thick slices are easy to cut into bite-sized pieces. You can buy ham steaks in smaller packs (shown above) or use a package of fully cooked, boneless, sliced ham (as I did in the recipe below).
  • Bone-in ham steak: This works just as well once you’ve removed the bone and any tough rind. Just remember that you need 1 lb. of usable ham, so you’ll need to buy a little extra to account for the weight of the bone.

What About Other Types of Ham?

I saw a LOT of ham options at the store when shopping for this recipe, and you probably will too, especially around the holidays. The fully cooked, boneless spiral-cut ham and smoked shank portion ham shown above can both work, BUT I’d save those bigger hams for a holiday centerpiece (like our baked ham) and use the leftovers for this soup.

I wouldn’t splurge on a big ham for this recipe unless you plan to use it for several meals. Ham slices are cheaper, easier, and all you need for this quickie soup.

Ham hocks are a different story! They’re better suited to traditional bean soups that simmer for hours. The long simmer gives the connective tissue time to soften and the smoky flavor time to develop. The pork hocks pictured above are only partially cooked, so they aren’t suitable for this shortcut recipe as written. If you’d prefer to make soup using a ham hock and dried beans, check out our navy bean soup recipe instead. It uses a smoked ham hock and takes over two hours of cooking, plus soaking time for the beans.

Print Add to Collection Go to Collections

Ham and Bean Soup

This incredibly easy and deliciously chunky Ham and Bean Soup features a medley of colorful vegetables, browned ham, and plenty of hearty white beans. Course Dinner, Lunch, Main Course, Soup Cuisine American Total Cost $11.47 recipe / $2.87 serving Prep Time 15 minutes minutes Cook Time 40 minutes minutes Total Time 55 minutes minutes Servings 4 servings (1.5 cups each) Calories 385kcal Author Beth Moncel

Equipment

  • Small Blender
  • Dutch Oven

Ingredients

  • 1 yellow onion medium dice, (2 cups) $0.95
  • 3 carrots medium dice, (2 cups) $0.75
  • 3 ribs celery medium dice, (2 cups) $0.61
  • 2 garlic cloves minced, (1 Tbsp) $0.12
  • 1 lb. cooked ham medium dice, $5.92*
  • 1 Tbsp cooking oil $0.04
  • 3 15.5 oz. cans cannellini beans $2.76**
  • ¼ tsp dried thyme $0.02
  • ¼ tsp black pepper freshly cracked, $0.04
  • 2 cups chicken broth or more as needed, $0.26***
US Customary – Metric

Instructions

  • Gather and prepare all ingredients. Dice the onion, peel and slice the carrots, slice the celery, and mince the garlic.
  • Dice the ham into bite-sized chunks. Add the ham and cooking oil to a large soup pot. Sauté the ham for 3-5 minutes over medium heat, or until it achieves a decent amount of browning. Remove the browned ham to a clean bowl.
  • Add the onion, carrots, celery, and garlic to the pot in place of the ham. Sauté the vegetables for about 5 minutes over medium heat, or until the onions have softened. Allow the moisture released from the vegetables to help dissolve the browned bits of ham from the bottom of the soup pot as you stir.
  • While the vegetables are sautéing, add one of the three cans of beans to a blender, with the liquid from the can, and purée until smooth. Drain the remaining two cans of beans.
  • Add all three cans of beans (one puréed and two drained) to the soup pot with the vegetables. Also add ¼ tsp dried thyme, ¼ tsp pepper, and stir to combine.
  • Next, stir in 2 cups chicken broth. Then turn the heat up to medium-high and allow the soup to come to a boil.
  • Once it reaches a boil, turn the heat down to medium and allow the soup to continue to boil for 15 minutes, stirring occasionally. As the soup boils, it will reduce and thicken. If the soup becomes too thick, add more chicken broth or water to achieve your desired soup consistency.
  • After the soup has boiled for 15 minutes and has thickened, stir the cooked ham back into the soup. Give the soup a taste and adjust the salt or pepper to your liking. I did not add any additional salt, but I did top each bowl with a little fresh pepper. Serve hot!

See how we calculate recipe costs here.

Notes

*Thicker slices work best for this soup, so ham steaks, leftover holiday ham or honey glazed ham are all good options. I used honey-smoked, fully cooked ham that I diced myself. Make sure you’re using fully cooked ham for this quick-cooking recipe. A raw ham hock or uncooked ham bone needs a different cooking method. **Canned cannellini beans are great here because they’re large, soft, creamy, and already cooked. Other white beans, like canned Great Northern or navy beans, work too. This recipe uses three 15.5 oz. cans, one puréed with its liquid and two drained and kept whole.  ***I use Better Than Bouillon to make my broth because it’s economical and easy to keep on hand. The saltiness of your broth will vary by brand, so hold off on adding extra salt until the soup is finished. You can also add more broth or water if you prefer a thinner soup. Seasonings: I kept the seasonings simple on purpose, choosing thyme and black pepper to complement the savory ham without needing a long simmer to develop flavor. Feel free to adjust the seasonings to your taste, but I’d skip anything that benefits from a longer cooking time, like bay leaves. This soup only simmers for 15 minutes! Can I use dry beans? I developed this recipe specifically for canned beans because they’re already cooked and make this soup so much faster! Dried beans take much longer to cook and require different amounts of liquid and seasoning. I haven’t tested this stovetop recipe with dried beans, but if you don’t have canned beans, here are two options to try:
  • -Cook the dried beans first: Soak the beans overnight, discard the soaking water, then boil vigorously in fresh water for at least 10-15 minutes and continue cooking until soft and the consistency of canned beans (this can take several hours). You’ll need about 4½ cups of cooked beans to replace the three cans. Purée 1½ cups with about ½ cup of water, then add the remaining 3 cups whole. This option means you can follow the recipe as written, only adjusting the broth as needed.
  • -Start with dried beans: I recommend following our navy bean soup instead. It uses soaked dried navy beans, ham hock, and simmers on the stovetop for about 2 hours. My old slow cooker ham and bean soup also uses soaked dried beans and ham hock (download that recipe here!). Or for a recipe that doesn’t need presoaked beans, try my slow cooker white bean soup, adding browned, fully cooked ham at the end.

Nutrition

Serving: 1.5cups | Calories: 385kcal | Carbohydrates: 39g | Protein: 34g | Fat: 13g | Sodium: 2226mg | Fiber: 12g

How to Make Ham and Bean Soup Step-by-Step Photos

Gather all of your ingredients: This is the type of ham I used (honey cured boneless ham). You can use any cooked ham, but a thicker slice works better than thin sandwich slices. You can also use cooked holiday ham. You’ll need 1 lb. of cooked ham.

Prep the veggies: Dice 1 yellow onion, peel and slice 3 carrots, slice 3 celery ribs, and mince 2 cloves of garlic. I recommend keeping the vegetables fairly uniform in size so they soften evenly during the short cooking time.

Brown the ham: Start by dicing 1 lb. of fully cooked ham into bite-sized pieces.

Add the ham and 1 Tbsp cooking oil to a large soup pot or Dutch oven. Sauté over medium heat for 3-5 minutes, stirring occasionally, until the edges turn golden brown. You should also see some browned bits developing on the bottom of the pot. Keep an eye on the heat so they don’t burn. Remove the ham to a clean bowl and leave those browned bits behind.

Sauté the vegetables: Add the prepared onion, carrots, celery, and garlic to the same pot. Sauté over medium heat for about 5 minutes, stirring occasionally, until the onions look translucent and the vegetables begin to soften. They’ll release some moisture as they cook, so use your spoon to gently scrape up the browned bits left by the ham.

Purée one can of beans: As the vegetables cook, add one 15.5 oz. can of cannellini beans to a blender, including all the liquid from the can. Blend until smooth and creamy. This mixture will help thicken the soup without adding cream!

Drain the other two cans: Now drain the remaining two cans of beans, but don’t purée them. We want those beans to stay whole for texture.

This is what the veggies should look like after sautéing for about 5 minutes. The bottom of the pot should also look noticeably cleaner as those bits dissolve into the vegetables.

Add the beans and seasonings: Add the puréed beans and both cans of drained beans to the pot with the softened vegetables. Sprinkle in ¼ tsp dried thyme and ¼ tsp freshly cracked black pepper. Stir gently until the creamy bean mixture coats the vegetables and whole beans. Try not to mash the whole beans as you stir.

Pour in the broth: Add 2 cups chicken broth and stir until everything is combined. The soup will look fairly watery at this point, but that’s normal! The puréed beans will give the broth more body as the liquid reduces during cooking.

Increase the heat to medium-high and bring the soup to a boil. Reduce the heat to medium and let it continue boiling for 15 minutes, stirring every few minutes to prevent the beans from sticking to the bottom of the pot. The broth should gradually reduce and become thicker and creamier. The vegetables should also be tender when pierced with a fork. If the soup gets too thick, add a splash of chicken broth or water until it reaches your preferred consistency.

Add the ham and serve: Once the soup has thickened, stir the browned ham back into the pot to warm through.

Give everything a taste before adding any salt, as the ham and chicken broth already contribute quite a bit! I didn’t need any extra salt in my batch, but I did add a little freshly cracked black pepper to each bowl. Serve hot and enjoy!

What to Serve with Ham and Bean Soup

This chunky ham and bean soup is a meal in a bowl! Between the ham, vegetables, and all those beans (which are super filling), you really don’t need much else to make a satisfying weeknight dinner. That said, I almost always serve some bread with my soup…it’s a must. 😋 A crusty baguette, sliced sourdough, or soft dinner rolls from the grocery store are all easy options. But my personal favorite here is a warm slice of cornbread. Its slightly sweet flavor pairs perfectly with the smoky, salty ham, and its tender, crumbly texture soaks up the creamy broth. If you want something fresh on the side, I’d go with a simple side salad. The crisp vegetables and tangy dressing offer a nice contrast to the warm, hearty soup, and it’s easy to throw together while the soup simmers.

Shown here with a side of cornbread and a simple salad.

Storage & Reheating

This ham and bean soup makes great leftovers. Let it cool quickly, then transfer it to airtight containers and refrigerate within 2 hours. It’ll keep in the refrigerator for 3-4 days. The soup will naturally thicken as it sits because the beans will keep absorbing moisture.

Reheating

I like to reheat this soup on the stovetop over medium heat, stirring occasionally until it comes to a boil and is heated all the way through (165°F). Add a splash of chicken broth or water as it warms to loosen the consistency. You can also reheat individual servings in the microwave, stirring partway through so they heat evenly.

Freezing

This soup freezes really well, too! Once cooled, divide it into individual portions in freezer-safe containers or bags. I prefer to divide my soup into single servings before freezing, so they can be reheated as needed and in the amount needed. Quart-sized freezer bags are great for freezing soup, as are the small blue-top Ziploc food storage containers. Freeze for up to 3 months for the best quality.

Got More Leftover Ham? Try These Recipes:

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

This baked fried chicken breast is the one you want when you’re craving crispy, golden fried chicken but don’t want a pot of hot oil on the stove. Thin chicken cutlets soak in buttermilk, get a coat of seasoned flour with paprika and savory all-purpose seasoning, and then bake in a pan of melted butter until the crust turns deep golden and crunchy. The inside stays juicy and tender, and the outside tastes like it came out of a fryer. You only need a handful of pantry ingredients and one baking sheet, so it works on a busy weeknight just as well as a Sunday supper. Serve it with mashed potatoes and biscuits for a classic fried chicken dinner, or tuck it into a bun for a crispy chicken sandwich. This is still my favorite way to get that fried chicken crunch at home.

5-Star Review

“This is absolutely delicious. I make crispy chicken sandwiches using this recipe, and they literally taste like PDQ Chicken. Yum!” -Anne

The Coating Fix That Makes This Recipe Work

I still vividly remember developing this baked fried chicken recipe. We were newly married and didn’t have a lot of money to eat out, and I wanted crispy, restaurant-worthy chicken, but at home and somewhat healthier. This seasoned and breaded chicken that’s oven-baked until crispy came to life, and I’ve been making it ever since. It’s one of my husband’s favorite recipes to this day, and now it’s a favorite for our two boys too.

Almost every fried chicken recipe you’ll find fries the chicken in oil. This one bakes on a thin layer of melted butter instead. The butter crisps the bottom of the crust while the oven browns the top, so you get that crunchy shell without the splatter, the oil thermometer, or the leftover pot of grease. If you’d rather use your air fryer, Air Fryer Fried Chicken gets a crunchy cornflake crust with no oil bath either.

I soak the chicken in buttermilk first, which is a common step in Southern fried chicken. Its mild acidity helps tenderize the meat so it stays juicy. Some recipes call for an overnight soak, but 20 minutes is enough for this quick version. You can absolutely go longer if you plan ahead.

After hearing from some of you who had trouble with the breading falling off, I retested the recipe to find a simple, reliable way to help the coating stick. Looking closely at your feedback, one pattern stood out. The pieces that kept their breading were the ones that went into the flour still damp with buttermilk. That buttermilk is the glue, and drying it off is what makes the coating slide away at the flip. So the recipe now leaves the chicken damp, gives the coating a short rest to set, and tells you exactly how to flip it.

Why Did My Breading Fall Off?

This is the most common question on this recipe, and the fix comes down to three small moments.

  • Before you dredge. If you blot the chicken dry, the flour has nothing to stick to. Let the extra buttermilk drip off for a few seconds, but leave the surface damp. That thin wet layer turns into the paste that holds the crust on.
  • After you dredge. Flour that goes straight into the oven stays dusty and flakes off. Let the coated chicken sit for 10 minutes. You’ll see the flour darken and turn paste-like, and that’s your sign it’s ready to bake.
  • At the flip. If the crust sticks to the pan, it isn’t ready yet. Hot butter helps the bottom set quickly, so heat the butter in the pan while the oven preheats. Then use a thin metal spatula and slide it all the way under each piece before you turn it. A fork or tongs will pull the coating right off.

Tips for Beginners

  • Leave the chicken damp, not dry. This is the step that matters most. A damp surface helps the seasoned flour grab on and bake into one solid crust. If the chicken is too dry, the coating won’t stick, and you’ll lose it the moment you flip.
  • Give the coating time to set. Ten minutes on a plate after dredging makes a big difference. The flour soaks up a little buttermilk and turns into a paste, so it bakes into a crunchy shell instead of a powdery layer that falls away.
  • Trust a thermometer, not the clock. Thin cutlets can cook through before the crust looks fully golden, and thicker ones can need a few extra minutes. The chicken is done when the thickest part reads 165°F. Pull it then, and it stays juicy.
Print

Baked Fried Chicken

This oven-fried chicken breast bakes up perfectly crispy on the outside, tender inside, and is an easy choice for quick, delicious dinners! Course Main Course Cuisine American Difficulty Easy Prep Time 25 minutes minutes Cook Time 40 minutes minutes Total Time 1 hour hour 5 minutes minutes Servings 4 servings Calories 402kcal Author Becky Hardin

Equipment

  • Baking Sheet

Ingredients

  • 2 boneless, skinless chicken breasts
  • 3 cups buttermilk
  • 1 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1 tbsp ground paprika
  • 1 tbsp all-purpose seasoning such as Lawry's
  • 1 tsp kosher salt
  • 1 tsp ground black pepper
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter melted (½ stick)
  • ¼ cup fresh flat leaf parsley optional

Instructions

  • Cut the chicken breasts in half, lengthwise, to create 4 thinner, even portions.
  • Place chicken in a large bowl and marinate in the buttermilk for at least 20 minutes, or up to overnight in the fridge.
  • Mix together the flour, paprika, all-purpose seasoning, salt, and pepper in a medium-sized bowl. Set aside.
  • Preheat oven to 400ºF. Place parchment paper on a 9×13-inch baking sheet, add the butter, and set the pan in the oven until the butter melts and bubbles. Tilt to coat the bottom of the pan.
  • Lift each piece of chicken from the buttermilk and let the excess drip off, but do not pat it dry. Press both sides firmly into the flour mixture and set on a plate.
  • Let the coated chicken rest for 10 minutes, until the flour looks damp and paste-like.
  • Place the chicken in the hot butter, making sure there is a small amount of room between each piece. This will allow them to crisp while baking.
  • Bake for 20 minutes. Flip each piece with a thin metal spatula, sliding it fully under the crust. Bake 15-20 minutes more, until golden brown and the thickest part reaches 165°F.
  • Let rest 5 minutes, then transfer to a serving dish. Garnish with parsley if desired. Enjoy!

Video

Notes

  • The buttermilk left on the chicken is what holds the coating in place, so let the excess drip off but don’t blot it dry. A dry surface gives the flour nothing to grab, and that’s when the breading slides off in the pan.
  • Flour that goes straight into the oven tends to flake off at the flip, so rest the coated chicken for 10 minutes before baking. That short rest turns the flour into a paste that bakes into one crunchy crust.
  • A crust that sticks to the pan usually isn’t ready to turn yet. Slide a thin metal spatula fully under each piece before flipping, and if it resists, give it a few more minutes. Heating the butter in the pan first also helps the bottom set faster.
  • Thin cutlets can finish cooking before the crust looks done, so check for 165°F in the thickest part instead of going by color alone. Pulling the chicken right at 165°F keeps it from drying out.
  • All-purpose seasoning is a savory blend of garlic, onion, paprika, parsley, salt, and pepper, like Lawry’s. If you can’t find it, use 1 tsp each of garlic powder, onion powder, and dried parsley instead. If your store-bought blend is mostly salt, cut the kosher salt in half to keep the sodium in check.
  • To make your own buttermilk, stir 3 tbsp of lemon juice or white vinegar into just under 3 cups of milk and let it sit for 5 minutes. It thickens slightly and works the same way in the soak.
  • Thighs, drumsticks, and wings all work with this coating. Bone-in pieces need more time, so bake until the thickest part reaches 165°F, which usually takes 45-50 minutes for thighs and drumsticks.
  • Leftovers keep in an airtight container in the fridge for up to 3 days and in the freezer for up to 3 months. To keep the crust crisp, reheat in the oven or air fryer instead of the microwave, which turns the breading soft.

Nutrition

Serving: 1serving | Calories: 402kcal | Carbohydrates: 35g | Protein: 22g | Fat: 19g | Saturated Fat: 11g | Polyunsaturated Fat: 1g | Monounsaturated Fat: 5g | Trans Fat: 0.5g | Cholesterol: 86mg | Sodium: 843mg | Potassium: 572mg | Fiber: 2g | Sugar: 9g | Vitamin A: 1852IU | Vitamin C: 6mg | Calcium: 240mg | Iron: 2mg

How to Make Oven-Fried Chicken Breasts Step by Step

Gather all the ingredients together.

Prepare the chicken: Cut 2 boneless, skinless chicken breasts in half lengthwise so you get 4 even, thinner portions about ½ inch thick.

Soak in buttermilk: Place the chicken in a large bowl and pour 3 cups of buttermilk over it. Marinate for at least 20 minutes, or cover and refrigerate up to overnight.

Mix the coating: In a shallow bowl, whisk together 1 cup of all-purpose flour, 1 tbsp of ground paprika, 1 tbsp of all-purpose seasoning, 1 tsp of kosher salt, and 1 tsp of ground black pepper. The mixture should look evenly rusty orange with no streaks of plain flour.

Heat the butter: Preheat the oven to 400°F. Line a 9×13-inch baking sheet with parchment paper, add 4 tbsp of unsalted butter, and set the pan in the oven while it heats. Once the butter melts and starts to bubble, tilt the pan so it coats the whole surface.

Bread the chicken: Lift each piece from the buttermilk and let the excess drip off for a few seconds, but don’t pat it dry. Press it firmly into the flour mixture on both sides, then set it on a plate.

Let the coating set: Rest the coated chicken for 10 minutes. The flour should look damp and paste-like with almost no dry white spots. If you see a dry patch, press on a little more flour.

Bake: Carefully place the chicken in the hot butter, leaving a little space between each piece so it can crisp instead of steam. You should hear a soft sizzle. Bake for 20 minutes, until the bottom is golden and releases easily from the pan. Slide a thin metal spatula all the way under each piece and turn it over in one motion. If a piece sticks, give it 2-3 more minutes and try again. Bake for another 15-20 minutes, until the crust is deep golden and crunchy and the thickest part reads 165°F.

Garnish and serve: Transfer the chicken to a serving dish and let it rest for a few minutes. Sprinkle with ¼ cup of chopped fresh flat-leaf parsley, if you like, and enjoy!

How to Store Baked Fried Chicken

Store leftovers in an airtight container or resealable bag, and keep them in the refrigerator for up to 3 days. I recommend wrapping each piece in foil or layering pieces with paper towels to soak up any moisture that might collect in the fridge. This helps keep the breading nice and crispy.

Freezer

To freeze, wrap each piece of chicken in foil, then place the pieces in a freezer-safe, resealable bag. Freeze for up to 3 months. Thaw in the fridge overnight before reheating. The crust won’t be quite as crunchy as the day you made it, but a hot oven brings most of the crispness back.

Reheating

  • Oven. This is my favorite way. Place the chicken on a baking sheet and reheat at 350°F for about 15 minutes, until it’s hot all the way through.
  • Air fryer. Reheat at 350°F for 5 to 7 minutes, flipping once. This is the fastest way to get the crust crunchy again.
  • Microwave. It works in a pinch, but the breading will turn soft. Use it only if you’re slicing the chicken for a wrap or salad.

Make Ahead

You can soak the chicken in buttermilk up to 24 hours ahead and keep it covered in the fridge. Coat it right before baking, since the flour gets gummy if the breaded chicken sits too long. You can also mix the seasoned flour a few days ahead and store it in an airtight container.

What to Serve With Baked Fried Chicken

This easy chicken breast recipe goes with any and all of your favorite side dishes, but when I’m in the mood for a classic fried chicken dinner, I go all in.

My go-to is a big bowl of Easy Mashed Potatoes. I boil the potatoes in milk, so they come out extra creamy, and that soft, buttery texture is the perfect match for the crunchy crust. I start the potatoes right after the chicken goes into the oven, and everything is ready at the same time.

For something green, I make Creamed Green Beans. They come together on the stovetop while the chicken bakes, and the Parmesan cream sauce makes the whole plate feel like a Sunday supper. They’re also gluten-free, which helps if you’re using a gluten-free flour blend for the chicken.

And don’t forget homemade Buttermilk Biscuits with a little Honey Butter. Since you already have buttermilk open for the soak, I always make a batch, and the sweet honey butter next to salty, crispy chicken is my boys’ favorite part of the meal.

When my kids are eating, I slice the chicken into strips and set out a bowl of Homemade Ranch Dressing for dipping. Leftovers also make a great crispy chicken sandwich with pickles and a soft bun.

Creamed Green Beans

Buttermilk Biscuits

Homemade Honey Butter Recipe

Weeknight Dinners With the Same Oven-Crisp Crunch

  • Panko Chicken: Chicken breasts get a coating of panko and Parmesan and bake to a crisp, golden finish in about 15 minutes. It’s a faster option for nights when there’s no time for a buttermilk soak.
  • Crispy Baked Chicken Fingers: Chicken breast strips get a crunchy coating and bake in the oven instead of a fryer. They’re a natural fit for kids who love dipping as much as eating.
  • Cheddar Bacon Ranch Chicken Tenders: Ranch seasoning, cheddar, and bacon flavor the panko coating on these oven-baked tenders. They make an easy game day dinner or party snack that still skips the deep fryer.

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

These Olive Chive Cream Cheese Rolls deserve a spot at the top of your baking list. Perfectly soft with an herbaceous cream cheese center and topped with green olives, these rolls are basically a mix between a dinner roll and a savory danish. They come together simply, and even an entry-level baker can whip up a batch effortlessly. We hope you love them as much as we do! 

Table of Contents

  • Why You’ll Love This Recipe
  • Ingredients You’ll Need
    • For the dough:
    • For the cream cheese filling:
    • For assembly:
    • Equipment Needed
  • Tips for the Best Results
  • Serving Suggestions
  • Storage Instructions
  • Recipe FAQs
  • More Bread Recipes We Think You’ll Love
  • Olive Chive Cream Cheese Rolls Recipe

Why You’ll Love This Recipe

  • Approachable for everyone. If you can mix and knead dough, you can make these! They’re easy to make at any baking skill level.
  • Perfectly savory. The herby cream cheese center really hits the spot. 
  • Impressive presentation. While these olive chive cream cheese rolls aren’t a hassle to make, they look like they’re fresh from a bakery or cafe. 
  • The options are endless. Use your favorite olives, add your favorite herbs, or switch up the flavor with roasted garlic or sun-dried tomatoes. The possibilities are endless!
  • Feeds a crowd. One recipe makes eight large rolls, making them perfect for brunch or a dinner party. 

Ingredients You’ll Need

For the dough:

  • Vegan butter – We like to use the country-crock plant butter sticks for this recipe rather than a dairy-free buttery spread from a tub. 
  • Non-dairy milk – Lukewarm milk helps activate the yeast and leaven the dough. We like to use soy milk or almond milk, but any unsweetened plant milk will do.
  • Granulated sugar – This feeds and activates the yeast. Use organic sugar if you’d like to ensure it’s not processed through bone char. 
  • Active dry yeast – Helps the dough rise and stay nice and fluffy. Always use yeast that isn’t expired. Expired yeast will not activate, and your dough may not rise. 
  • All-purpose flour – For the most accurate results, we recommend measuring your flour in grams instead of cups, so if you have a food scale at home, now is the time to use it. 
  • Salt – Helps balance the flavor in the savory dough, 
  • Vegan parmesan – Folds into the dough to add a unique savory flavor to the rolls. We like to freshly grate the Violife Foods parmesan wedge rather than using a pre-grated vegan parm. 

For the cream cheese filling:

  • Vegan cream cheese – Make sure it softens up at room temperature before making the filling. This will help ensure it’s smooth and lump-free. We like to use the Violife Supreme Cream Cheese block or the dairy-free Philadelphia cream cheese. 
  • Cornstarch – Helps firm up the filling so the cream cheese doesn’t run and the rolls stay intact. 
  • Vegan parmesan – Adds a subtle, salty, nutty depth to the filling. 
  • Fresh chives – Brings a bright onion flavor to the filling. We recommend fresh chives over dried. 
  • Black pepper – Adds the perfect peppery hint to the filling. We recommend freshly cracked pepper for the best flavor. 

For assembly:

  • Non-dairy milk and olive oil – Whisk these two together and brush over the unbaked dough so it gets perfectly golden in the oven.
  • Olives – Grab a 10 oz jar of your favorite variety. Castelvetrano are buttery and mild, Kalamata are punchy and briny, and an olive medley gives you a bit of both. Make sure the pits are removed. 
  • Chopped chives – For garnishing the finished rolls. 

Equipment Needed

  • Large mixing bowl
  • Medium mixing bowl
  • Kitchen scale
  • Baking sheet
  • Parchment paper
  • Pastry brush
  • Piping bag (optional)
  • Flat bottom cup

Tips for the Best Results

  • Check the milk temperature. Use a thermometer to make sure your milk is between 105-110ºF; if it’s too hot, it will kill the yeast and the dough won’t rise. 
  • Don’t skimp on the kneading. Knead the dough for 10 minutes to fully develop it. You’ll know it’s ready when it’s smooth and bounces back slightly when you poke it.
  • Do not overfill the rolls. Fill each with about 2 tablespoons. Adding more than that may cause the filling to spill out while baking.
  • Dry your olives. Remove excess brine to keep the filling from loosening too much. 
  • Allow them to cool fully. Straight out of the oven, the filling will be scorching hot. Let them rest for about 15 minutes to help the rolls set properly. 

Serving Suggestions

These are great with brunch or dinner. They pair perfectly with a big bowl of soup or alongside our Brussels Sprout Caesar Salad. You can also serve them with our Tofu Scramble for a hearty brunch with friends. These Olive Chive Cream Cheese Rolls are also perfect on a holiday appetizer table. Of course, you can also eat them straight from the tray for any anytime snack. 

Storage Instructions

  • Refrigerator: Keep cooled rolls in an airtight container for up to 4 days. Because the filling contains cream cheese, we don’t recommend leaving these out at room temperature. 
  • Freezer: Freeze baked and fully cooled rolls in a single layer, then transfer to a freezer-safe bag for up to 2 months.
  • Reheating: Reheat in a 325ºF oven for 8 to 10 minutes, or until warmed through. From frozen, thaw overnight in the fridge first. We do not recommend the microwave, since it can make the dough gummy. 

Recipe FAQs

Can I make the dough ahead of time?

Yes, you can! Once the dough rises the first time, cover it tightly and refrigerate overnight. Allow it to come back to room temperature for about an hour before dividing and shaping into balls.

Why didn’t my dough rise?

More often than not, the problem is expired yeast or milk that was too hot. After 10 minutes of activating, your yeast should look foamy. If it doesn’t, repeat the process instead of continuing with the recipe.

Can I use a stand mixer instead of kneading by hand?

You can! Use the dough hook on medium-low for about 6 to 8 minutes, until the dough is smooth and pulls away from the sides of the bowl.

What if I do not like olives?

Swap them for halved cherry tomatoes, sun-dried tomatoes, roasted red pepper strips, or caramelized onions. The cream cheese base works with all of them.

Can I make these gluten-free?


We have not tested a gluten-free version, and enriched yeast doughs are tricky to convert. If you experiment with a one-to-one gluten-free bread flour blend, let us know how it goes.

Olive Chive Cream Cheese Rolls

5 Stars 4 Stars 3 Stars 2 Stars 1 Star

No reviews

  • Author: Chris Petrellese
  • Total Time: 2 hours 50 minutes
  • Yield: 8 rolls 1x
  • Diet: Vegan
Print Recipe Pin Recipe

Description

These Olive Chive Cream Cheese Rolls deserve a spot at the top of your baking list. Perfectly soft with an herbaceous cream cheese center and topped with green olives, these rolls are basically a mix between a dinner roll and a savory danish. They come together simply and even an entry-level baker can whip up a batch effortlessly. We hope you love them as much as we do!

Ingredients

For the dough

  • 6 tablespoons melted vegan butter
  • 1 cup non-dairy milk, lukewarm
  • 3 tablespoons granulated sugar
  • 2 1/4 teaspoons active dry yeast (1 standard packet)
  • 3 1/2 cups (420g) all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 cup vegan parmesan

For the cream cheese filling

  • 8 ounces vegan cream cheese, softened
  • 1 tablespoon cornstarch
  • 1/4 cup vegan parmesan
  • 3 tablespoons chopped chives
  • Black pepper, to taste

For assembly

  • 2 tablespoons non-dairy milk
  • 1 tablespoon olive oil
  • 1 (10-ounce) jar olives of choice, pitted
  • Chopped chives, for topping

Instructions

  1. Activate the yeast. In a large bowl, add the melted vegan butter, lukewarm non-dairy milk, and sugar. Stir until dissolved, then add the yeast. Set aside for 8-10 minutes to allow the yeast to activate. It will appear foamy when it’s ready.
  2. Form the dough. In the same bowl, add the flour, salt, and vegan parmesan, and mix until uniform and a rough dough forms.
  3. Knead. Transfer the dough to a floured work surface. Knead for 10 minutes, adding more flour as necessary. The dough should be smooth and bounce back slightly when you poke it.
  4. First rise. Place the dough in an oiled bowl, toss to coat, and cover with a damp towel or plastic wrap. Set aside in a warm place to rise for about 1 hour, or until doubled.
  5. Make the filling. Meanwhile, add all the filling ingredients to a medium bowl and mix until well combined. Chill until ready to use.
  6. Divide and shape. Once the dough has doubled, transfer it to a clean work surface. Divide into 8 equal portions and shape each into a smooth ball.
  7. Second rise. Place the dough balls onto a parchment-lined baking sheet, spread evenly apart. Cover with a damp towel or plastic wrap and prove for 45 minutes.
  8. Preheat. Preheat the oven to 350ºF.
  9. Fill the rolls. Use a glass or flat-bottomed measuring cup to press down the center of each dough ball, creating a well with a border about 1/2 inch wide. Use a spoon or piping bag to add about 2 tablespoons of the cream cheese mixture to each roll. Be careful not to overfill, or the cream cheese will overflow.
  10. Add the olives. Press 2-3 olives into the cream cheese center of each roll.
  11. Brush with the wash. Mix together the non-dairy milk and olive oil in a small bowl, then brush onto the exposed dough of each roll.
  12. Bake. Bake for 30-35 minutes, or until golden brown. Allow to cool for at least 15 minutes.
  13. Finish and serve. Top with additional fresh chives and black pepper, then enjoy.

Notes

  • Milk temperature: Lukewarm means about 105 to 110ºF. If it feels hot to the touch, let it cool before adding the yeast.
  • Olive prep: Pat olives dry before pressing them in. Excess brine will loosen the filling.
  • Make ahead: After the first rise, refrigerate the dough overnight. Bring to room temperature for about an hour before shaping.
  • Storage: Refrigerate up to 4 days or freeze up to 2 months. Reheat at 325ºF for 8 to 10 minutes.
  • Prep Time: 2 hours 15 minutes
  • Cook Time: 35 minutes
  • Category: Appetizer, Baked Goods
  • Method: Oven
  • Cuisine: American
