📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

This one-pot chicken spaghetti is everything you love about the classic, with a creamy, cheesy sauce and tender, seasoned chicken in every bite. The difference is how it all comes together. Instead of boiling the noodles separately, the spaghetti simmers right in chicken broth and heavy cream along with browned chicken, onion, garlic, and Rotel. All that pasta starch stays in the pot, so once the cream cheese, sharp cheddar, Monterey Jack, and Parmesan melt in, you get a velvety sauce that hugs every strand. There’s no canned soup, no colander, and no casserole dish required. It’s cozy and rich, with just a little zip from the green chiles, and it’s exactly the kind of dinner I reach for on a chilly weeknight or when I’m feeding a hungry crowd.

Chicken Spaghetti Like You Remember, Just Better

Chicken spaghetti is one of those dinners that just feels like home. I grew up with the chicken spaghetti casserole version that always started with a can of cream of mushroom or cream of chicken soup. I was determined to create a version with fresh ingredients that keeps all the ease and simplicity of the recipe we all grew up with.

I’ve made a lot of versions over the years, but I wanted one that was truly a one-pot dinner. Most chicken spaghetti recipes cook the pasta separately and then combine everything in a casserole dish, and I wanted to see if I could get that same creamy, cheesy result completely on the stovetop. Cooking the spaghetti directly in the chicken broth ended up being the key. Instead of draining all that pasta starch away, it stays right in the pot and becomes part of the sauce. The chicken, Rotel, onion, and garlic all cook with the pasta, so every bite picks up the same savory flavor.

I tested the cheese combination with the same goal in mind. I wanted it creamy and cheesy without one cheese overpowering the rest. Sharp cheddar gives the sauce that familiar chicken spaghetti flavor, Monterey Jack keeps it smooth and melty, and Parmesan adds a little saltiness and depth to round it all out. Using chicken tenders keeps the chicken tender too, and it’s seasoned all the way through.

As a mom of four, recipes like this are essential to have in my back pocket. It takes about 10 minutes of prep and 30 minutes on the stove, and it makes 8 hearty servings. It uses ingredients I can easily keep on hand, and everything happens in one pot, which means no sink full of dishes waiting for me after dinner. You’ll know it’s done right when the sauce looks thick, glossy, and creamy, with just a little heat from the Rotel to keep it from feeling too rich. If it looks a little loose when you turn off the heat, don’t worry. It thickens up quite a bit as it rests.

Do I Need Cream of Chicken Soup?

Nope, and you won’t miss it. Classic chicken spaghetti leans on a can of condensed soup to make it creamy, but this version gets the same rich texture from three simple things working together.

  • Pasta starch. Because the spaghetti cooks right in the broth and cream, its starch stays in the pot and naturally thickens the sauce.
  • Heavy cream and cream cheese. The cream adds body while the pasta cooks, and the cream cheese melts in at the end for that velvety, tangy finish.
  • Freshly shredded cheese. Sharp cheddar, Monterey Jack, and Parmesan melt smoothly into the starchy base for a sauce that’s cheesy without being greasy.

The result tastes like the chicken spaghetti you remember, with a fresher flavor and a creamier texture. If you love the baked casserole style with a crispy topping, try my Baked Chicken Spaghetti instead.

Tips for Beginners

  • Keep it at a gentle simmer. This is the most important step in the whole recipe. A hard boil makes the liquid cook off before the spaghetti is tender, and that’s how one-pot pasta ends up dry, sticky, or gummy. You want small, steady bubbles around the edges of the pot. When the heat stays gentle, the pasta cooks evenly and releases its starch slowly into the broth, which is exactly what makes the sauce so creamy.
  • Stir every couple of minutes and don’t rinse the pasta. Spaghetti loves to clump together and stick to the bottom of the pot when it cooks in a small amount of liquid. A quick stir every 2 minutes keeps the strands separate and cooking at the same rate. And since the starch is what helps the sauce cling to every strand, never rinse or drain anything. All of that goodness belongs in the pot.
  • Shred your own cheese and add it over low heat. Pre-shredded cheese is coated with anti-caking powders that keep it from melting smoothly. Freshly shredded cheese melts into a silky sauce instead. Turning the heat down to low before the cheese goes in matters just as much, because high heat can make cheese sauces turn grainy. Add the cheese a handful at a time and let each one melt before adding the next.
Print

One-Pot Chicken Spaghetti

One-Pot Chicken Spaghetti is a creamy, cheesy dinner made with seasoned chicken, Rotel, and spaghetti cooked right in a rich chicken broth and cream sauce. It’s an easy, family-friendly weeknight meal with no canned soup, no draining, and just one pot to wash. Course Dinner Cuisine American Difficulty Easy Prep Time 10 minutes minutes Cook Time 30 minutes minutes Total Time 40 minutes minutes Servings 8 servings Calories 785kcal Author Haley Herridge

Ingredients

  • 2 lbs boneless skinless chicken tenders cut into bite-size pieces
  • 1 tbsp olive oil
  • 1 tsp kosher salt
  • 1 tsp garlic powder
  • 1 tsp onion powder
  • 1 tsp Italian seasoning
  • ½ tsp smoked paprika
  • ½ tsp black pepper freshly cracked
  • 1 medium yellow onion finely diced
  • 1 tbsp minced garlic
  • 1 10 oz. can Rotel diced tomatoes with green chiles undrained
  • 4 cups low-sodium chicken broth
  • 1 cup heavy cream
  • 16 oz. spaghetti
  • 2 tbsp butter
  • 8 oz. full-fat cream cheese cubed
  • 1½ cups freshly shredded sharp cheddar
  • 1 cup freshly shredded Monterey Jack
  • ½ cup freshly grated Parmigiano-Reggiano

Instructions

  • Heat the olive oil in a large, heavy-bottomed pot over medium-high heat. Season the chicken with the salt, garlic powder, onion powder, Italian seasoning, smoked paprika, and black pepper. 2 lbs boneless skinless chicken tenders, 1 tbsp olive oil, 1 tsp kosher salt, 1 tsp garlic powder, 1 tsp onion powder, 1 tsp Italian seasoning, ½ tsp smoked paprika, ½ tsp black pepper
  • Add the chicken and cook for 4-5 minutes, stirring occasionally, until lightly browned and almost cooked through. Transfer the chicken to a plate and set aside.
  • Add the onion to the same pot and cook for 3-4 minutes, until softened. Add the garlic and cook for about 30 seconds, until fragrant. 1 medium yellow onion, 1 tbsp minced garlic
  • Pour in the Rotel with its juices and stir for about 1 minute, scraping up the browned bits from the bottom of the pot. 1 10 oz. can Rotel diced tomatoes with green chiles
  • Add the chicken broth and heavy cream. Stir and bring to a gentle boil. 4 cups low-sodium chicken broth, 1 cup heavy cream
  • Add the spaghetti, pressing it under the liquid as it softens. Cover and cook at a gentle simmer for 10-13 minutes, stirring every couple of minutes, until almost al dente. 16 oz. spaghetti
  • Reduce the heat to low. Add the butter and cream cheese and stir until melted and smooth. 2 tbsp butter, 8 oz. full-fat cream cheese
  • Stir in the chicken and any juices from the plate. Cook for 2-3 minutes, stirring gently, until the chicken is cooked through.
  • Stir in the cheddar, Monterey Jack, and Parmigiano-Reggiano, letting each addition melt before adding the next. 1½ cups freshly shredded sharp cheddar, 1 cup freshly shredded Monterey Jack, ½ cup freshly grated Parmigiano-Reggiano
  • Let the pot rest off the heat for a few minutes so the sauce can thicken. Serve topped with parsley, green onions, extra Parmesan, and cracked black pepper.

Notes

  • The pasta needs liquid to cook through, and a rolling boil evaporates it too quickly, so keep the pot at a gentle simmer the whole time the spaghetti cooks. This is the difference between a creamy sauce and dry, gummy noodles.
  • Spaghetti cooked in a small amount of liquid tends to clump and stick, so stir every couple of minutes while it simmers. It also helps the strands cook evenly.
  • The starch from the pasta is what makes this sauce creamy, so never rinse or drain the spaghetti. Everything stays in the pot and becomes part of the sauce.
  • Pre-shredded cheese has coatings that keep it from melting smoothly, so shred the cheese yourself for the silkiest sauce. Adding it over low heat keeps it from turning grainy.
  • The broth, Rotel, and cheeses all bring plenty of salt, so taste before adding any more salt at the end. Using low-sodium broth helps keep this in check.
  • Original Rotel has a mild kick that most kids handle well. If you’re cooking for spice-sensitive eaters, use Mild Rotel instead, or choose Hot Rotel for more heat.
  • For a shortcut, you can swap in 4 cups of shredded rotisserie chicken. Skip browning, season the onion and garlic instead, and stir the chicken in after the cream cheese melts so it doesn’t dry out.
  • The sauce may look a little loose when you first turn off the heat, but let it rest for a few minutes before serving. It thickens up quite a bit as it sits.
  • Leftovers keep in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 4 days. The pasta soaks up sauce as it sits, so add a splash of broth or milk when reheating. This dish doesn’t freeze well, since the creamy sauce can separate and the spaghetti turns soft.

Nutrition

Serving: 1serving | Calories: 785kcal | Carbohydrates: 51g | Protein: 45g | Fat: 45g | Saturated Fat: 24g | Polyunsaturated Fat: 3g | Monounsaturated Fat: 13g | Trans Fat: 0.1g | Cholesterol: 216mg | Sodium: 856mg | Potassium: 711mg | Fiber: 3g | Sugar: 5g | Vitamin A: 1411IU | Vitamin C: 5mg | Calcium: 426mg | Iron: 3mg

How to Make One-Pot Chicken Spaghetti Step by Step

Gather all the ingredients together.

Season and brown the chicken: Heat 1 tbsp of olive oil in a large, heavy-bottomed pot over medium-high heat. Season 2 lbs of bite-size chicken tender pieces with 1 tsp of kosher salt, 1 tsp of garlic powder, 1 tsp of onion powder, 1 tsp of Italian seasoning, ½ tsp of smoked paprika, and ½ tsp of black pepper. Add the chicken to the pot and cook for 4-5 minutes, stirring occasionally, until it’s lightly browned on the edges and almost cooked through. Transfer the chicken to a plate and set it aside. You’ll add it back once the spaghetti is cooked, so save any juices that collect on the plate.

Build the classic chicken spaghetti base: Add 1 finely diced yellow onion and cook for 3-4 minutes, until softened and turning translucent. Stir in 1 tablespoon of minced garlic and cook for about 30 seconds, just until fragrant. 

Add the Rotel: Pour in one 10-oz can of Rotel with all its juices and stir for about a minute, scraping up the browned bits from the bottom of the pot. Those bits are packed with flavor.

Add the creamy liquid: Pour in 4 cups low-sodium chicken broth and 1 cup heavy cream. Stir everything together and bring it to a gentle boil.

Cook the spaghetti right in the sauce: Add 16 oz. spaghetti directly to the pot. As the noodles soften, after a minute or two, press them down under the liquid. Cover and cook for 10-13 minutes, stirring every couple of minutes. Keep the heat at a gentle simmer with small bubbles around the edges. The liquid will slowly turn creamier and thicker as the pasta releases its starch.

Melt in the butter and cream cheese: Once the spaghetti is almost al dente, with just a little bite left in the center, reduce the heat to low. Add 2 tbsp butter and 8 oz. cubed cream cheese and stir until completely melted and smooth.

Add the chicken back: Stir the browned chicken and any juices from the plate back into the pot. Let it cook over low heat for 2-3 minutes, stirring gently, until the chicken is cooked through.

Stir in the cheese: Add 1½ cups of sharp cheddar, 1 cup of Monterey Jack, and ½ cup of Parmigiano-Reggiano, letting each addition melt before adding the next. The sauce should turn thick, glossy, and incredibly creamy, coating every piece of chicken.

Rest and serve: Let the pot sit off the heat for a few minutes so the sauce can thicken. Serve topped with 2 tbsp of chopped fresh parsley and 2 thinly sliced green onions, plus extra Parmesan and cracked black pepper if you like.

How to Store and Reheat Chicken Spaghetti

Store leftovers in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 4 days. The spaghetti keeps absorbing the sauce as it sits, so it will look thicker the next day. That’s completely normal, and a splash of liquid brings it right back.

Freezer

I don’t recommend freezing this one. Cream cheese sauces tend to separate and turn grainy after thawing, and spaghetti that has already soaked up sauce gets soft and mushy. It’s best enjoyed fresh or as leftovers from the fridge.

Reheating

  • Stovetop. This is the best way to bring back the creamy texture. Add the leftovers to a pot with a splash of chicken broth or milk and warm over medium-low heat, stirring occasionally, until heated through.
  • Microwave. Add a splash of broth or milk, cover the bowl loosely, and heat in 1-minute intervals, stirring between each one so the sauce stays smooth.

Make Ahead

This recipe is best made fresh because the spaghetti continues to absorb the sauce as it sits. You can still do almost all of the prep earlier in the day. Chop the chicken and onion and keep them covered in the refrigerator, measure out the seasonings, and shred the cheese. When it’s time for dinner, everything is ready to go straight into the pot. Pre-chopped onion and rotisserie chicken make it even easier.

What to Serve with Chicken Spaghetti

I always serve this the classic way, straight from the pot with a big sprinkle of freshly grated Parmesan, lots of cracked black pepper, and a little extra parsley or green onion on top. Since it’s a complete meal with pasta, chicken, and a creamy sauce, I like to keep the sides simple.

My kids’ favorite part of this dinner is a warm piece of homemade garlic bread for scooping up every last bit of cheesy sauce left in the bottom of the bowl. When I want something a little more special for Sunday dinner, I make a batch of Olive Garden-style breadsticks instead.

With a sauce this rich, I always want something bright and crunchy on the side. A cool, tangy cucumber salad cuts right through the creaminess, and you can make it ahead so it’s ready the minute dinner is. On nights when I want a leafy salad, this green goddess salad is my go-to because its herby dressing pairs so well with the Rotel’s little bit of heat.

For a quick weeknight, I’ll roast a pan of broccoli or green beans while the pasta cooks. They need nothing more than olive oil, salt, and pepper, and the hands-off timing means dinner still comes together all at once.

More Nostalgic Chicken Comfort Food

It’s the perfect choice when you want the classic baked version that comes out of the oven hot, bubbly, and golden.
  • Easy Chicken Tetrazzini: Chicken tetrazzini is another creamy chicken pasta classic, made with fettuccine tossed in a rich garlic and mushroom cream sauce. A layer of Parmesan and buttery breadcrumbs bakes up golden on top, making it a cozy option for family dinners and potlucks.
  • Crockpot Chicken and Dumplings These slow cooker chicken and dumplings bring all the old-fashioned comfort of the classic, with fluffy biscuit dumplings, carrots, and peas in a creamy broth. The slow cooker does most of the work, which makes it a great fit for busy days when you still want a home-cooked meal.

Welcome to the weekend, my friends! We’ve had a bit of a sleepless week watching over our sweet Oscar and making sure he’s taking it as easy as possible, getting all of his meds, resting, and healing after his surgery on Tuesday. And I’m thrilled to tell you that he’s getting around well and learning to navigate again now that his leg has been amputated. So many of you have been messaging as I’ve shared his videos in my daily stories, and we appreciate the prayers and well wishes so much.

ON THE BLOGS THIS WEEK: Crisp warm tortillas filled with chicken, bell peppers, and plenty of melting cheese provide a terrific dinner, or an excellent way to use up just a bit of leftovers. Who would turn down piping hot Chicken Fajita Quesadilla for lunch or dinner? (Not me!)

Savory and sweet blend perfectly with a touch of heat in this sausage and apple Stuffed Acorn Squash. Although it also makes a great side, this fall classic has been the main event at our table more than a few times.

Jalapeno Corn Pudding is a sweet and savory cornbread with a spicy kick. With a golden brown crust and a soft, custard-like center, it’s American comfort food at its best. I’ve loved corn pudding for as long as I can remember. Adding jalapenos gives the casserole a little bit of heat that I find irresistible.

Light, crispy, and flaky Air Fryer Brussels Sprouts Chips are delightfully snackable and surprisingly satisfying. These chips are the snack you never knew you were missing!

Spaghetti Pancakes are a fun treat made by drizzling batter onto the griddle in long, skinny strands. They take a little more effort than a regular stack, but they’re a great way to do something different on a weekend morning.

You only need two ingredients and about five minutes to make this Easy Hot Fudge Sauce. This is a dream come true (and a “must have in the house at all times” requirement) for any ice cream lover!

Thin, chewy, buttery Oatmeal Lace Cookies with crispy edges have proven completely and totally irresistible to every single person who has tried them. What makes a cookie a lace cookie? If you hold the cookie up to the light, it’s nearly thin enough in places to see through it. Yet, it’s chewy, and crispy, and oh so delicious, not at all fragile once cooled.

What I’m CRAVING: Christi’s Chocolate Peanut Butter Cup Cookies. Because once you’ve tasted them, there is no other cookie option for satisfying a chocolate peanut butter craving. They are unreal!

My FAVORITE THINGS this week are the elevated dog bowls we bought for Oscar. It’s such an incredibly simple thing, but wow, what a huge difference it has made! We realized he wasn’t drinking water because he couldn’t easily get to the bowl without slipping forward on his front legs. It is more work to balance on just the one rear leg now. (He doesn’t seem to mind doing that for his food, but can’t be troubled for the water. Ha.) With the elevated bowls, he’s eating and drinking like a champ again! YAY!

What I’m READING: Once again, I’m all about the comfort reads. I started Anne of Green Gables last night and it’s been making me smile all over again. In case you don’t already have it on your Kindle, the full 8 book set is still a $0.99 (yes, less than one dollar!) download and that continues to thrill me all these years later. Send me more of your comfort reads, friends!

What I’m WATCHING: The Life List on Netflix. This is a feel-good tearjerker, and also a fun romantic comedy. Was it a bit predictable and formulaic? Sure. But, I still really enjoyed it!

“For I, the Lord your God, hold your right hand; it is I who say to you, “Fear not, I am the one who helps you.” Isaiah 41:13 ESV

Wishing you a beautiful week filled with little things that make you smile!

If there’s one thing I appreciate when cooking dinner for my family, it’s a meal that doesn’t leave me with a mountain of dirty dishes! That’s why I’m such a big fan of sheet pan dinners. Most of the cooking happens right in the oven, so there’s less time spent standing over the stove and more time to get on with everything else. And you can do SO much with a simple baking sheet. From juicy chicken and roasted veggies to homemade pizza, crispy tacos, and meatless meals, I’ve rounded up 13 easy sheet pan dinners that’ll help you save time, money, and a whole lot of washing up!

Chicken Sheet Pan Dinners Packed with Flavor

Chicken is one of the easiest meats to find at the grocery store, and there are loads of ways to prepare it. It’s also a great choice for sheet pan dinners because it cooks relatively quickly and takes on all sorts of delicious seasonings and sauces. Winner winner chicken dinner!

1. Sheet Pan Chicken Fajitas

Sheet Pan Chicken Fajitas

These easy Sheet Pan Chicken Fajitas are a simple hands-off way to get that smoky-sweet flavor of traditional griddle fajitas. 4.79 from 415 votes $10.19 recipe / $2.55 serving Get the Recipe

Who says you need a sizzling skillet to make fajitas?! These sheet pan chicken fajitas have all those delicious smoky spices, tender chicken, and roasted peppers and onions, but with far less hands-on cooking. I’d serve these family-style, placing the sheet pan in the middle of the table with warm tortillas and everyone’s favorite toppings. That way, everyone can build their fajitas exactly how they like them.

Our readers have had fun switching up the vegetables, too, adding everything from broccoli to parsnips and butternut squash. Just don’t skip that honey mustard sauce! It’s what brings everything together.

Pound the chicken breasts to an even thickness before roasting so they’re ready at roughly the same time as the veggies.

I’d look for an inexpensive store-brand pesto and freeze any leftovers in small portions for future meals. It’s a simple way to stretch one jar across several dinners!

A takeout favorite, made easier in the oven (say LESS!) This family-friendly sheet pan sweet and sour chicken has juicy pineapple, bell peppers, onions, and bite-sized chicken pieces, all tossed in a homemade sticky, tangy sauce. My family absolutely looooves this one! I also appreciate that the oven roasts everything while you prepare the sauce, instead of managing a fast-moving stir-fry. Spoon it over fluffy rice, and dinner is sorted before you know it.

BBQ Favorites

There’s something just so good about BBQ sauce once it’s been heated in the oven. The sugars start to caramelize, creating a glossy, sticky coating with extra flavor around the edges. And you don’t need to fire up the grill to get those delicious barbecue flavors! These easy sheet pan dinners turn two family favorites, meatballs and meatloaf, into simple sheet pan meals with minimal cleanup.

You can fit everything onto one sheet pan, although I’d use two if you have them to give the veggies more room to caramelize. Add a side of rice, and you’ve got four satisfying servings that are great for meal prep, too.

Instead of one large loaf, this sheet pan BBQ meatloaf recipe makes two individual beef meatloaves with a sticky barbecue topping. Sweet potatoes and frozen broccoli roast on the same pan, so you’ve got your main and two sides taken care of. I especially like that you can cook the vegetables straight from frozen, saving time and reducing waste! 🥦

Make It a Sheet Pan Night

One of my favorite ways to keep the grocery bill down is to recreate our favorite takeout orders at home. It’s much cheaper than ordering in, and using a sheet pan makes some of those traditionally hands-on meals surprisingly easy!

They’re filled with seasoned ground turkey, refried beans, and Monterey Jack cheese, with a creamy dipping sauce on the side. You can use corn or flour tortillas, depending on what you’ve already got. Just warm them first, so they fold without breaking!

And at around $1.41 per serving, this sheet pan pizza is a budget-friendly alternative to ordering pizza. The dough does need time to rise, so I’d plan ahead for this one.

10. Sheet Pan Doner Kebab

Just mix ground beef with grated onion, garlic, and savory spices, roll it thin in parchment paper, then bake and broil for those delicious crispy edges. Pile it into warm pita with homemade tzatziki and fresh veggies, and you’ve got a takeout-style dinner in about 30 minutes.

Meatless Options

Skipping meat a few nights a week is one of the easiest ways to bring down your grocery bill! Ingredients like tofu and mushrooms can make incredibly filling meals without the cost of meat (I promise!). And if you’re looking for an easy place to start, these meatless sheet pan dinners make the switch pretty irresistible.

13. Sheet Pan Mushroom Steak Dinner

One Pan “Mushroom Steak” Dinner

This vegan One Pan Mushroom Steak Dinner has juicy portobello mushroom steaks, roasted potatoes, and fresh veggies all cooked together on one sheet pan. 5 from 2 votes $12.27 recipe / $3.06 serving Get the Recipe

Since I’m already talking mushrooms…I couldn’t leave out my one pan mushroom steak dinner! I use a simple marinade of balsamic, soy sauce, garlic, and thyme to give the portobello caps an incredibly savory flavor. I’ve timed the roasting so the potatoes go in first, then the mushrooms, followed by the asparagus and tomatoes. Everything finishes cooking together, so you don’t have to juggle separate pots or watch sides get cold while you wait. It’s a complete, satisfying meatless dinner made on one sheet pan!

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Jalapeno Corn Pudding is a sweet and savory cornbread with a spicy kick. With a golden brown crust and a soft, custard-like center, it’s American comfort food at its best.

I’ve loved corn pudding for as long as I can remember. Adding jalapenos gives the casserole a little bit of heat that I find irresistible.

Jalapeno Corn Pudding

Growing up, my mom made corn pudding for almost every holiday meal or special occasion. It’s a tradition I’ve carried on with my own kids, although I make my corn pudding from scratch instead of from a boxed mix.

Jalapeno Corn Pudding has all the nostalgic creamed corn flavor of the original but with a spicy twist. The heat from the jalapenos gives a satisfying balance to the sweet corn without lighting your mouth totally on fire. I love this so much that I’ve even been known to sneak bites of the leftovers cold, straight from the fridge! I hope it brings the same level of joy to your table.

Ingredients and Substitutions

Corn – You’ll need both whole kernel canned corn and creamed corn.

Dry Ingredients – I use cornmeal, along with all purpose flour, baking powder, and salt to give the pudding structure.

Eggs and Sugar – These create the custard-like consistency and enhance the natural sweetness of the corn.

Sour Cream and Butter – I use full fat sour cream for moisture and tang, along with salted, melted butter for rich flavor.

Jalapenos – You can use any jarred or canned jalapenos to add heat and spice.

Notes on Equipment

  • Chef’s knife. Food prep is so much easier when you have a quality knife! I first tried this knife 10 years ago, while visiting a professional test kitchen and you could say it was love at first slice. (And I was thrilled to discover how reasonably priced they are!) That’s why mine has been in my kitchen ever since.
  • Cutting board
  • Large mixing bowl
  • Spatula or spoonula
  • Measuring cups and spoons
  • 9×13 Baking dish. I adore Pyrex dishes. They’re one of those kitchen staples that that just makes cooking easier. And, I can vouch for how sturdy and durable they are (I’ve had some of mine almost 20 years). And, who doesn’t love a dish that comes with a tight-fitting lid?

How to Make Jalapeno Corn Pudding

Preheating the Oven: Set your oven temperature to 350°F. While my oven preheats, I grease a 9 x 13 pan.

Preparing the Jalapenos: Drain the jalapenos from the jar and chop them into small pieces.

Combining the Eggs and Sugar: You’ll add the eggs and sugar to your mixing bowl. Whisk them together until well combined.

Adding the Wet Ingredients: Next, I add the sour cream, followed by the creamed corn, canned corn kernels, butter, and jalapenos. Give the mixture a good stir after adding each ingredient.

Mixing in the Dry Ingredients: We’ll add the flour, cornmeal, baking powder, and salt to the corn mixture. Stir until well combined.

Baking the Corn Pudding: I pour the batter into my greased baking dish and place the baking dish in the oven to bake until golden brown, about 50 minutes.

Cooling and Serving: I let the it cool for about 10 minutes before serving. You can serve in square slices or scoop it onto plates with a big serving spoon.

Expert Tip

The finished dish has the very mildest of heat, just a hint really. That said, if you want a dish that will make everyone at the table sit up and take notice, you can substitute diced green chiles with fantastic results. The canned green chiles will give the corn pudding an earthier, smokier heat that pairs beautifully with creamy corn – and you can choose anything from mild to extra hot heat levels too.

Bake Times May Vary – Ovens all have distinct “personalities” that can affect your overall baking time. The first time you make the recipe, check it after 50 minutes of baking. If the top is still pale and the center looks runny, leave it in the oven for another 5 minutes, then check again.

Serving Suggestions

Jalapeno corn pudding pairs well with southwest flavors like Green Chile Chicken Cordon Bleu or Pan Fried Southwest Chicken. And it’s also terrific with a BBQ potluck spread of Pulled Pork, Memphis Style Coleslaw, and Slow Cooker Baked Beans.

Storage and Reheating

How to Store: Store leftovers tightly covered in the refrigerator for up to 2 days or in the freezer for up to one month. You can also freeze the entire casserole or divide it into individual portions for easy reheating.

How to Reheat: Reheat individual servings in the microwave until warmed through. You could also reheat the entire pan, covered with foil, in the oven. Thaw (if frozen), cover the pan with foil, and reheat at 300°F until warm.

More Corn Dishes

  • Crockpot Creamed Corn
  • Creamed Corn with Peppers and Bacon
  • Jalapeno Corn Coleslaw
  • Elote Dip
  • Street Corn Pasta Salad

Frequently Asked Questions

Can I use Jiffy Mix instead of cornmeal?

Not in this recipe. Jiffy cornbread mix has flour and other added ingredients, so it won’t work as a one-to-one substitute here.

Can I leave out the sugar?

The sugar really is necessary here. It not only adds sweetness to the pudding, it’s also responsible for some of the texture. Sugar binds to the egg yolks creating the consistency you recognize in custards and baked cheesecakes.

How can I tell when corn pudding is fully cooked?

You want the pudding to look lightly golden brown on top and a bit firm. The center should still have some “give” to it but not be runny like cake batter.

Can I make this gluten free?

You bet! Just substitute 4 tablespoons of brown rice flour and 2 tablespoons of potato starch for the all-purpose flour in this recipe. Alternatively, you can simply substitute 1:1 with the GF blend of your preference, if you already have a favorite.

Print

Jalapeno Corn Pudding

This savory sweet jalapeno corn pudding is an American classic side dish. Kick it up to a new level with the addition of spicy peppers. Course Side Dish Cuisine American Prep Time 10 minutes minutes Cook Time 50 minutes minutes Total Time 1 hour hour Servings 12 servings Calories 212kcal Author Mary Younkin

Ingredients

  • 4 eggs
  • ½ cup sugar
  • ½ cup sour cream
  • 15 ounces canned creamed corn
  • 15 ounces whole kernel corn drained
  • 4 ounces canned or jarred jalapenos diced small, about ½ cup
  • 4 tablespoons butter melted and slightly cooled
  • ½ cup all-purpose flour plus 2 tablespoons
  • 6 tablespoons cornmeal
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • ¼ teaspoon kosher salt

Instructions

  • Preheat the oven to 350 degrees. Grease a 9×13 baking dish and set aside.
  • Whisk together the eggs and the sugar and then add in the sour cream, creamed corn, whole corn, jalapenos, and butter, stirring between each addition.
  • Add the flour, cornmeal, baking powder, and salt to the corn mixture. Stir until well combined.
  • Pour into the prepared pan and bake for 50-55 minutes, depending on your pan. When the pudding is golden brown and slightly firm to the touch, it is done.
  • Let cool at least ten minutes before serving. Scoop with a spoon or slice into squares to serve.

Notes

* Chopped green chiles may be substituted for the jalapenos in this recipe for extra heat.

Nutrition

Calories: 212kcal | Carbohydrates: 33g | Protein: 5g | Fat: 8g | Saturated Fat: 4g | Cholesterol: 70mg | Sodium: 313mg | Potassium: 223mg | Fiber: 2g | Sugar: 11g | Vitamin A: 308IU | Vitamin C: 3mg | Calcium: 52mg | Iron: 1mg

Has Substitution Culture Gone Too Far?

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

This excerpt was originally published in Pre Shift, our newsletter for the hospitality industry. Subscribe for more first-person accounts, advice, and interviews.

When Chanita Chayaluk decided to open her restaurant and wine bar Taan, she hoped “to showcase Thai food in Chicago in a different light,” she says. “The first [wave of] immigration of Thai people in Chicago was in the 1970s, and a lot of [Thai] food here still feels like it’s from that time.” In September, she opened with a menu including miang, a Northern Thai dish made with fermented tea leaves, and a whole, deep-fried fish in a fish-sauce caramel. 

Another quirk of operating in Chicago? “We’re still pretty ‘Midwest nice,’” says Chayaluk. “We try to overaccommodate and appease the masses.” In the early days of service, guests made frequent requests for substitutions or accommodations due to preferences and dietary restrictions. “It would slow down service and speed, and that was not just affecting us, but the guest experience in many ways,” she says. With a small kitchen and staff, it was hard to keep up. “It just felt like we were Chipotle.” When a guest asked for green curry mussels “but without the mussels,” it was a turning point. 

Chayaluk implemented a limited substitution policy at Taan, stating on Instagram that ingredients like fish sauce and shrimp paste are “the fabric that defines our cuisine,” and therefore, could not be removed from any dishes that include it. 

A decade or so ago, substitution culture was generally frowned upon and derided. Recently, though, some restaurants report it bubbling back up—and in full force. There’s a swirl of factors that got us here: Lunch slop bowl spots primed customers to expect a detailed level of customization with their orders. Unreasonable Hospitality pushed an intensified “customer is always right” mentality. And influencers entering the food-review space have made criticism of anything—including a restaurant choosing not to honor certain dietary preferences—ripe for an immediate, viral takedown. Couple all of that with the fact that it is hard to keep a restaurant afloat these days, and you’ve got several operators giving in, whether to specific requests, or to altering their menus altogether (see also: the great de-spicification of menus.)

At the same time, restaurants like Taan and New York’s Ha’s Snack Bar—which proudly announces in its Instagram bio that everything contains fish sauce—are standing their ground.

For Lom Wong in Phoenix, Arizona, substitution and modification requests have ramped up after receiving national attention. Chef Yotaka “Sunny” Martin was recognized by the James Beard Awards last year, and the restaurant was named a Michelin Bib Gourmand in August. “[After the award,] we had more guests than we were used to picking things apart. They were asking us to modify dishes or not understanding our service style and why we weren’t coursing things in a European fashion,” says chef and co-owner Alex Martin. “[There is a] European distinction of what an award-winning restaurant looks like, so people equated that recognition with a white-tablecloth restaurant. We were just like, ‘Well, that’s not who we’ve ever been, and it’s never what our restaurant will ever be.’”

Like Taan, Lom Wong took to social media to set expectations, including that the restaurant would not “make substitutions simply to make a dish more familiar,” and that certain dishes might differ from Western expectations of them; beef might have gristle, fishes will be whole, and both choices are fully intentional. “Sunny is our guiding light of cultural context,” says Martin. “[She advises, considering if] changing something about a dish compromises the integrity of the dish.” For example, the mango salad can be ordered with the shrimp on the side, but the restaurant won’t remove beef or shrimp paste from dishes that necessitate them. 

Chayaluk takes a similar approach at Taan; there is always a vegan, gluten-free option (currently a fried rice) that she describes as a “catchall” for those who can’t have other items on the menu. “We make sure that that dish is also really, really good and substantial; it’s not just an appetizer,” she says. “It’s not an afterthought.” 

Staff at both restaurants are encouraged to uphold the boundaries, which both Chayaluk and Martin say are as much in place for protecting their staff’s peace as they are for ensuring quality control for guests. While there has been some backlash, the expectation-setting at the start of the meal (and before, thanks to the social media posts) has for the most part been taken well. “The way we explain [our policy] to guests is kindly saying we respect your preferences, but they’re your preferences,” says Martin, “and steering them toward dishes on the menu that might already align with those.”

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Chicken pot pie is one of my favorite dishes, but dealing with two crusts can be such a chore. This chicken pot pie casserole makes my life so much easier! Instead of canned soup, it starts with a homemade filling. Butter, veggies, broth, cream, and a splash of white wine cook down into a silky, savory gravy. It’s loaded with tender chicken, carrots, peas, and mushrooms. Then it bakes under one flaky crust until the top is golden and the filling bubbles. This crazy good chicken casserole is the ultimate easy comfort food, and it tastes like you spent all day on it.

5-Star Review

“I love this pot pie! So savory with the wine and seasoning…It is delicious! Just made it again this week! Second time this fall! Love your step by step instructions.” -Susan

Pot Pie vs. Pot Pie Casserole

This casserole has everything I love about a classic pot pie. It’s loaded with the tastiest sauce, chunky chicken, peas, carrots, and more, and topped with a beautiful lattice pie crust. I love how homey, delicious, fun, and comforting it is. It has all the flavor of my favorite pot pie, but it’s there for me when I need something less fussy, just like this chicken pot pie soup with pie crust crackers.

Here’s what makes the casserole version so much easier.

  • Only one crust. A classic pot pie needs a bottom crust and a top crust, and that bottom layer can turn soggy under a creamy filling. Here the filling goes straight into the dish, and the crust only goes on top, so there’s nothing to blind bake and nothing to go soggy.
  • Less hands-on time. Prep only takes 15 minutes, and it’s so unfussy! Because it uses cooked chicken, the filling comes together in one skillet in about the time it takes the oven to preheat.
  • Easier to serve. There’s no slicing neat wedges out of a pie plate. You just scoop it from the dish with a big spoon, and every serving gets plenty of crust and creamy filling.
  • Made for a crowd. One 2-quart dish makes 8 hearty servings, so it feeds the whole family with room for leftovers.

If you love a bottom crust, you can still add one. Use a second pie crust and prebake it before adding the filling so it doesn’t turn out raw or gummy underneath.

One Crust Is All You Need

The filling is fully cooked on the stovetop before it ever hits the oven. That means the crust is only there to bake up flaky and golden, and a single top crust does that job perfectly. Skipping the bottom crust keeps every bite crisp on top and creamy underneath, without any doughy layer at the bottom of the dish.

  • Store-bought is totally fine. A refrigerated 9-inch pie crust makes this a true weeknight dinner. Just let it sit on the counter for 15 to 20 minutes before you roll it out. Cold dough cracks when you try to bend it, and a few minutes at room temperature makes it soft and easy to work with.
  • Want to make your own? My homemade pie crust is simple, tender, and flaky, and it’s made with shortening, so it’s easy to roll out (and dairy-free too). You only need the single-crust batch for this recipe.
  • Make the crust ahead. Homemade dough can be shaped into a disc, wrapped tightly in plastic wrap, and kept in the fridge for up to 3 days or in the freezer for up to 3 months. Thaw frozen dough overnight in the fridge, then let it warm up on the counter before rolling. On busy days, I like to have a crust ready to go so dinner is just filling, crust, and bake.

Tips for Success

  • Simmer until it’s truly thick. The filling should look like a thick stew and coat the back of a spoon before it goes into the dish. It won’t thicken much more in the oven, so if it looks soupy, give it a few more minutes on the stove. If you like your filling even thicker, keep simmering until it reaches the consistency you want.
  • Cook the flour for the full 3 minutes. Stirring the flour into the butter and veggies for a few minutes cooks out the raw flour taste and gives it the power to thicken the gravy. Rushing this step is the most common reason a pot pie filling turns out runny.
  • Keep the crust taut. Try to keep the crust relatively taut over the baking dish so that it doesn’t touch the filling. If it is sitting directly on the filling, it may take longer to bake due to the extra moisture.
  • Watch the color. If you notice that the crust is browning too quickly, loosely cover the casserole with foil halfway through baking.
  • Let it rest before serving. Give the casserole about 10 minutes out of the oven before you scoop it. The filling sets up as it cools slightly, so it holds together instead of running all over the plate.
Print

Chicken Pot Pie Casserole

A creamy, from-scratch chicken pot pie filling loaded with tender chicken, carrots, peas, and mushrooms, baked under one flaky golden pie crust. You get all the comfort of a classic pot pie with no bottom crust and no canned soup. Course Main Course Cuisine American Difficulty Easy Prep Time 15 minutes minutes Cook Time 1 hour hour Total Time 1 hour hour 15 minutes minutes Servings 8 servings Calories 439kcal Author Becky Hardin

Equipment

  • 2-Quart Baking Dish

Ingredients

  • 1 9" pie crust store bought or homemade
  • 4 tbsp unsalted butter (½ stick)
  • 2 tbsp olive oil
  • 2 carrots peeled and diced
  • 2 ribs celery diced
  • 1 sweet yellow onion peeled and minced
  • 2 cloves garlic minced or pressed
  • 3 cups cubed cooked chicken
  • 4 oz. button mushrooms cleaned and diced
  • 1 cup frozen peas thawed
  • ¼ cup all-purpose flour
  • 2 cups low-sodium chicken broth or stock
  • ¼ cup white wine semi-dry or Chardonnay
  • 1 cup heavy cream or half-and-half
  • ½ tsp kosher salt or to taste
  • 1 tsp freshly ground black pepper
  • ½ tsp dried thyme
  • ¼ tsp ground sweet basil
  • 1 large egg beaten slightly with 1 tsp water

Instructions

  • Preheat oven to 375°F and spray a 2-quart casserole dish with nonstick spray. Set aside. Allow the pie crust to warm at room temperature for 15-20 minutes before rolling out. 1 9" pie crust
  • Heat the butter and oil in a skillet over medium-high heat until they shimmer. 4 tbsp unsalted butter, 2 tbsp olive oil
  • Add diced carrots and celery and cook for 3 minutes. Add minced onion and cook until onion is translucent. Add minced onion and cook until onion is translucent. 2 carrots, 2 ribs celery, 1 sweet yellow onion, 2 cloves garlic
  • Add the cooked chicken, diced mushrooms, and peas and cook 1 minute. 3 cups cubed cooked chicken, 4 oz. button mushrooms, 1 cup frozen peas
  • Sprinkle vegetables and chicken with the flour and cook 3 minutes, stirring continuously. ¼ cup all-purpose flour
  • Stir in chicken broth and scrape the bottom of the pan to deglaze the pan. 2 cups low-sodium chicken broth
  • Pour in the white wine and cream. Stir well. ¼ cup white wine, 1 cup heavy cream
  • Season to taste with salt and add the pepper, thyme, and basil. Stir well and continue to cook until thickened to a thick-stew consistency. ½ tsp kosher salt, 1 tsp freshly ground black pepper, ½ tsp dried thyme, ¼ tsp ground sweet basil
  • Pour chicken mixture into the prepared casserole dish.
  • Roll the pie crust to ¼-inch thickness and cut into 1-inch strips to create a lattice top. Or, drape it over the filling, trim or crimp the sides, and cut a few small vents.
  • Combine egg and water and brush the pastry with the egg mixture. 1 large egg
  • Bake on the center rack of the oven for 35-45 minutes, or until the top is golden. Rest 10 minutes before serving.

Video

Notes

  • This recipe is built for leftovers, so the chicken only warms through in the filling instead of cooking from raw. Use any cubed or shredded cooked chicken, like rotisserie chicken, leftover roast chicken, or even leftover turkey. Raw chicken won’t have time to cook through on the stovetop, so cook it first if that’s what you have.
  • The butter and olive oil both have a job here, so melt them together before adding the veggies. Butter gives the filling its rich flavor, while the oil keeps the butter from scorching over medium-high heat. Together they give the flour enough fat to soak up for a smooth, lump-free gravy.
  • This filling should be thick before it ever goes in the oven, so simmer until it coats the back of a spoon like a thick stew. It won’t thicken much more as it bakes. If you use half-and-half instead of heavy cream, plan on a few extra minutes of simmering since it’s a little thinner.
  • For a gluten-free pot pie, swap in a gluten-free pie crust and a cup-for-cup gluten-free flour blend. The blend thickens the gravy the same way all-purpose flour does, so the rest of the recipe stays the same.
  • For a dairy-free pot pie, use dairy-free butter and coconut cream or a soy-based heavy cream. Check that your pie crust is dairy-free too. My homemade crust is made with shortening, so it already is.
  • The wine adds a touch of acidity that keeps this rich filling from tasting heavy. If you’d rather skip it, replace it with more chicken broth and a small splash of vinegar to keep that same brightness.
  • Leftovers keep tightly covered in the fridge for up to 3 days and in the freezer for up to 2 months. If you plan to freeze it, bake or store it in a metal or foil pan instead of glass, since glass can shatter when it goes from the freezer to a hot oven.

Nutrition

Serving: 1serving | Calories: 439kcal | Carbohydrates: 22g | Protein: 20g | Fat: 30g | Saturated Fat: 14g | Polyunsaturated Fat: 3g | Monounsaturated Fat: 11g | Trans Fat: 0.2g | Cholesterol: 111mg | Sodium: 328mg | Potassium: 432mg | Fiber: 3g | Sugar: 4g | Vitamin A: 3403IU | Vitamin C: 10mg | Calcium: 60mg | Iron: 2mg

How to Make Chicken Pot Pie Casserole Step by Step

Gather the ingredients: Gather all the ingredients together. Preheat the oven to 375°F and spray a 2-quart casserole dish with nonstick spray. Take your 9-inch pie crust out of the fridge and let it sit at room temperature for 15-20 minutes. Cold dough cracks when you roll or bend it, and a little time on the counter makes it soft enough to work with.

Melt the butter and oil: Heat 4 tbsp of unsalted butter and 2 tbsp of olive oil in a large skillet over medium-high heat until the butter melts and the fat starts to shimmer. The oil keeps the butter from browning too fast, so you get all that buttery flavor without any burnt bits.

Build the veggie base: Add 2 diced carrots and 2 diced ribs of celery and cook for 3 minutes. They go in first because they’re the firmest and need a head start. Add 1 minced sweet yellow onion and cook until it turns soft and translucent. Then stir in 2 cloves of minced garlic for 30 seconds, just until fragrant. Garlic burns quickly, which is why it goes in last. This carrot, celery, and onion trio is the classic base of any pot pie, and it gives the gravy a sweet, savory backbone.

Add the chicken, mushrooms, and peas: Stir in 3 cups of cubed cooked chicken, 4 oz. diced button mushrooms, and 1 cup thawed frozen peas, and cook for 1 minute. The chicken is already cooked, so it only needs to warm up. The mushrooms add a rich, meaty umami flavor that makes the filling taste deeper, and the peas bring little pops of sweetness and color.

Cook the flour: Sprinkle ¼ cup of all-purpose flour over everything and cook for 3 minutes, stirring the whole time. The flour soaks up the butter and oil and turns into a paste that coats the chicken and veggies. Cooking it the full 3 minutes gets rid of the raw flour taste and gets it ready to thicken the gravy. Everything should look evenly coated, with no dry white streaks of flour left.

Deglaze with broth: Pour in 2 cups of low-sodium chicken broth and scrape the bottom of the pan with a wooden spoon. The browned bits stuck to the skillet are packed with flavor, and the broth lifts them right into the gravy. Low-sodium broth lets you control the salt since the filling reduces as it simmers. It will look thin at this point, and that’s normal.

Add the wine, cream, and seasonings: Stir in ¼ cup of white wine and 1 cup of heavy cream. The cream makes the filling rich and velvety, and the wine adds a little acidity so all that richness doesn’t feel heavy. Season with ½ tsp of kosher salt, 1 tsp of freshly ground black pepper, ½ tsp of dried thyme, and ¼ tsp of ground sweet basil. The thyme is what gives it that classic pot pie flavor.

Simmer until thick: Keep cooking and stirring until the filling thickens to a thick-stew consistency. It should coat the back of a spoon and slowly fill back in when you drag a spoon through the pan. Don’t rush this part, because the filling won’t thicken much more in the oven. Taste it and add more salt if needed.

Fill the casserole dish: Pour the filling into the prepared casserole dish and spread it into an even layer so the crust sits flat on top.

Top with the crust: Roll the pie crust to about ¼-inch thickness. Cut it into 1-inch strips and weave a lattice over the filling, or drape the whole crust over the top and trim or crimp the edges. If you use a full crust, cut a few small slits in the center so steam can escape. Keep the crust as taut as you can over the dish so it bakes up crisp instead of sinking into the filling.

Brush on the egg wash: Beat 1 large egg with 1 tsp water and brush it over the crust. The egg wash is what gives the top that shiny, deep golden-brown finish.

Bake until golden: Bake on the center rack for 35-45 minutes, until the crust is golden brown and the filling is bubbling around the edges. If the crust browns too fast, loosely tent it with foil. 

Rest and serve: Let the casserole rest for about 10 minutes before serving so the filling can set.

How to Store, Freeze, and Reheat

Store leftover chicken pot pie casserole in an airtight container (or tightly covered in its baking dish) in the refrigerator for up to 3 days. The crust will soften a bit overnight, but the oven brings back some of its crispness.

Freezer

You can freeze chicken pot pie casserole both before and after baking. Just be sure to do so in a non-glass baking dish (a metal or foil pan works great) to prevent it from shattering. Tightly wrap the casserole in 2 layers of plastic wrap and 1 layer of aluminum foil and freeze for up to 2 months. The cream sauce may look slightly separated after thawing, but it comes back together as it heats.

Reheating

  • Oven (best for leftovers). Reheat refrigerated leftovers in a 375°F oven for 25 to 30 minutes, or until warmed through. Cover loosely with foil if the crust starts getting too dark.
  • Microwave (best for single servings). Heat a scoop in 30-second bursts, stirring the filling between rounds. The crust won’t be crisp, but it’s the fastest option for lunch.
  • Already-baked frozen casserole. Place it in a 375°F oven for 30 to 35 minutes.
  • Unbaked frozen casserole. Bake in a preheated oven with the foil on for 30 minutes, then remove the foil and continue to bake for 45 to 50 minutes, until golden brown and 165°F in the center.

How to Make Chicken Pot Pie Casserole Ahead+

Cook the filling as directed, then let it cool completely. Store it covered in the fridge for up to 2 days. When you’re ready to bake, spread it in the dish, top with the crust, and bake as usual.

Assemble the Night Before

Let the filling cool completely before you add the crust. Hot filling steams the dough from underneath and can leave it gummy. Once it’s assembled, cover the dish and refrigerate until you’re ready to bake. Brush on the egg wash right before it goes in the oven, and add an extra 5 to 10 minutes to the bake time since it’s starting cold.

Freeze It Unbaked

Assemble the casserole in a freezer-safe pan with cooled filling, skip the egg wash, and wrap it as described in the freezer section. Either thaw it overnight in the fridge and bake as usual, or bake it straight from frozen using the reheating directions above. This is a great meal to drop off for a new parent or a friend who needs a hand, because they can bake it whenever they’re ready.

Serving Suggestions

This chicken pot pie casserole is a meal all by itself, and most nights I just scoop it straight from the dish into shallow bowls so everyone gets plenty of crust and gravy. It’s my go-to when I’m feeding the whole family on a busy weeknight, hosting a casual Sunday supper, or bringing dinner to a friend. One dish makes 8 hearty servings, so it feeds a crowd without me juggling a bunch of pans.

Because the filling is so rich and creamy, I like to round it out with a green vegetable side or two. Roasted Parmesan broccoli with lemon is the side I make most often with this casserole. The bright lemon cuts right through the creamy gravy, and I can prep it while the casserole bakes. If you’re making a dairy-free pot pie, just skip the Parmesan.

When I want a second veggie with a little more texture, I make roasted Brussels sprouts. The crispy, caramelized edges are a nice contrast to the soft, creamy filling, and they feel right at home on a cozy fall or winter table.

For a heartier side that still feels fresh, I make green beans with bacon. The smoky, slightly sweet beans pair naturally with the savory gravy, and it’s the side I reach for when I’m serving this for Sunday supper or having company over.

For bigger crowds or really hungry appetites, I spoon the pot pie over a scoop of make ahead mashed potatoes. The creamy filling acts just like gravy, and since the potatoes are made in advance, it’s an easy way to stretch dinner for a holiday weekend or a houseful of guests.

Roasted Parmesan Broccoli with Lemon

Make Ahead Mashed Potatoes

Green Beans with Bacon

More Scratch-Made Casseroles 

  • King Ranch Chicken Casserole: This Tex-Mex casserole layers cooked chicken with a creamy homemade green chile sauce, two cheeses, and crushed tortilla chips instead of the usual canned soup and Velveeta.
  • Chicken Divan: Leftover or rotisserie chicken and tender broccoli bake in a homemade cheddar cream sauce under a buttery panko topping. The whole casserole can be assembled and refrigerated up to 2 days before baking.
  • Turkey Rice Casserole: Leftover holiday turkey, rice, peas, and carrots simmer in a creamy broth and cream sauce before baking under a crunchy cracker crumb topping. It’s a natural next step for anyone who swaps turkey into this pot pie casserole after Thanksgiving.

The ‘Tavern-Style’ Pizza Takeover

📅 Oct 20, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →
The thin-crust pizzas popular throughout the Midwest have taken off as the “tavern-style” pizza trend | Brad Bobrow/La Merna

In the suburbs of Chicago where they both grew up, it was just pizza, according to Brittany and Brennan Grosvenor, the couple behind Proof in Portland, Oregon. Ordering came down to the question of “thin-crust, deep-dish, or double-dough,” Brittany recalls of her family’s Friday night tradition. The Grosvernors sell the former at Proof, though the language of “thin crust” has given way to the more nationally familiar phrasing of “tavern-style,” the pizza trend that has swept from coast to coast in recent years. 

Outsiders might associate Chicago with its famous, tourist-beckoning deep-dish pizza, but Brittany says that’s just not reflective of reality, citing deep dish as a much less frequent treat than the Midwestern, thin-crust style that’s gaining popularity. “The pizza you get every week, or if you go to a kid’s birthday party, it’s always tavern-style,” she says.

“Tavern-style” pizza — which is prevalent  across the Midwest, with Chicago, Milwaukee, Minnesota, and St. Louis touting similar pies — is known for its thin, cracker-like crust, cut into a lattice instead of triangular slices. Depending on the region and the pizza shop where one’s nostalgia lies, the sauce might lean sweet or spicy. Lightness and crispness, though, is the style’s unique sell. Writing for Esquire in 2023, Omar Mamoon called the variant, which was then still on the rise, “a foil” to famously thick Detroit-style pizza, which had its big moment in the late 2010s. 

The beauty of the tavern-style pie is its shareability. A pizza yields more slices when cut into a grid than into triangles, explains Bradley Shorten, who’s behind both Kim’s Uncle Pizza in his hometown Chicago suburb of Westmont, Illinois, and the recently opened La Merna in Washington, D.C. Pizza cut into little squares makes for great drinking food, especially since it fits on a cocktail napkin. What makes a good pie, according to Shorten, is that every square has a little of every ingredient; the toppings go to the edge. And while the four, small corner slices might not be the “best” ones, Shorten says, they’re the ones “everybody goes for,” especially if they’re from Chicago.

Over the past few years, as “taverns” have become growing fixations for a cadre of restaurateurs, the “party cut” pizza and tavern-style pizza spots have also become ubiquitous, with openings like Keepers in New York city; One Trick Pony in Forth Worth, Texas; Tillie’s Tavern Pizza in Wheat Ridge, Colorado; and Zarella and Dimmi Dimmi Corner Italian in Chicago, where the style has always been abundant. For some Chicagoans, these openings across the country are an opportunity to correct the record about their city’s pizza culture, and for other Midwesterners to claim their stake in the region’s pizza, too.

When Mike Fadem opened the second location of his popular NYC pizzeria Ops — known for its wood-fired Neapolitan-esque pies with leopard-spotted crusts and pliant dough — he wanted to offer something new. Fadem is from St. Louis, where the city’s namesake pizza is super-thin. Despite his love for it, “it’s maybe not the best version of tavern pizza,” Fadem admits, noting the iteration’s many critics, some of whom take issue with its use of the gooey processed cheese called provel. The trajectory of tavern-style pizza has been like a game of telephone, he explains: St. Louis pizza is derived from thin-crust Chicago pizza, which itself came from Neapolitan pizza. Divergence has accompanied each jump.

One of Fadem’s employees at Ops, meanwhile, is from New Jersey, which claims its own thin-crust, tavern- or bar-style pizza tradition. (The bar pies of the Boston area also have their own devoted following.) Accordingly, “I had this interest in ‘can we make an Ops version of this?’” Fadem says. With Ops known for sourdough, Fadem also wanted to highlight the rich flavor of whole grains. And he wanted it to be really crispy, a quality he says draws in New Yorkers. Fadem’s take is on the menu as the regionally agnostic “tavernetta.” Though St. Louis and New Jersey served as initial inspiration, the best pizza from his research was actually in Milwaukee. The Tavernetta hasn’t displaced the other pies at the restaurant; it’s ordered in addition to them, with most groups getting at least one.

In June, Los Angeles cocktail spot Bar Next Door celebrated its third anniversary by adding food to its menu. Previously, the bar had allowed customers to bring in pies from Prince Street Pizza next door since Prince Street co-owner Lawrence Longo is a partner in it. “But, you know, after having a slice, sometimes you get a little full and you’re like, I want to go to bed,” says owner and bar director Brynn Smith. “That’s the whole goal for us: for people to stay a little longer, drink more. We thought the tavern-style pizza would be perfect.” The fact that the pizzas — they’re technically listed on the menu as “bar pies,” to better go with the establishment’s name — are made exclusively for the bar by Prince Street just adds another reason for people to go out, Smith says.

The idea of the tavern as a social and convivial space is inseparable from the tavern-style pizza. Shorten’s goal with La Merna — which he runs with his wife Meg Schmitt, who grew up in St. Louis — was the feel of a Chicago dive bar or a Wisconsin supper club: a place with wood-paneled walls and checkered floors, where people could slide into a red leather booth for pitchers of beer and pizza and hang out for a while. As my colleague Francky Knapp wrote last year, the rising appeal of the “tavern” vibe across restaurants nationwide is rooted in the sense of providing somewhere homey and lived-in. At taverns, diners are beckoned to sprawl out and stay awhile; to free themselves of pretense and to tap into something welcoming in its familiarity. This, for so many people, is exemplified by the archetype of hometown pizza shop, which is forever tethered in our cultural imaginations to the trappings of a previous, simpler era.

It’s for this reason that, in Oregon, the Grosvenors have started serving their take on Bosco sticks, the stuffed breadsticks of Midwestern cafeterias, and Italian beef with giardiniera. While they could find certain Midwestern hits around Portland, it sometimes frustrated them that they couldn’t find everything in one place. According to Brennan Grosvenor, “We’re just trying to embody the feeling: If you went into a tavern in Chicago, what could you order there?”

Pastina Soup

This pastina soup recipe is the best kind of comfort food. Filled with tiny pasta (pastina, in Italian) and tender vegetables, it’s cozy and flavorful, but nourishing too. Some of the vegetables stay whole for texture, while the rest get blended into the broth, thickening it slightly and giving it a stunning golden color. On a chilly fall or winter day, it’s a delicious way to warm up. Pastina soup is sometimes called Italian penicillin. You can think of it like chicken noodle soup’s Italian cousin, a perfect soothing meal for days when you’re under the weather. Most versions call for chicken […]
📅 Oct 19, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

These Pumpkin Cheesecake Bars are the perfect fall sweet treat. They’re layered with a golden graham cracker crust, a tangy dairy-free cheesecake, and a delicately spiced pumpkin cheesecake on top. This easy and fuss-free dessert comes together with minimal effort. The hardest part is just waiting for the bars to set. When it’s finished, you’ll have a beautifully layered treat to share with your friends and family. 

Table of Contents

  • Why You’ll Love This Recipe
  • Ingredients You’ll Need
    • For the graham cracker crust:
    • For the cheesecake filling:
    • Equipment Needed
  • Tips for the Best Results
  • Serving Suggestions
  • Storage Instructions
  • Recipe FAQs
  • More Pumpkin Recipes Worth Trying
  • Pumpkin Cheesecake Bars Recipe

Why You’ll Love This Recipe

  • Easy no-bake filling. Coconut oil helps everything set in the fridge, so you won’t need a water bath or worry about your cake cracking. 
  • Two beautiful layers, one base recipe. Make a single cheesecake filling, divide it in half, and stir in pumpkin and spice to make 2 distinct layers. 
  • Beautiful presentation. After the bars set, you get 3 distinct layers when you slice them that will wow your guests. 
  • Perfect make-ahead dessert. Since these bars need at least 8 hours in the fridge, they’re perfect to make ahead of a holiday or party. 
  • Real pumpkin flavor. Just half a cup of purée is enough to taste and see while still keeping the filling light. 

Ingredients You’ll Need

For the graham cracker crust:

  • Vegan graham cracker sheets. Since most graham crackers contain honey, check the labels carefully. We like to use the classic Nabisco graham crackers in the red box, or the original Kroger brand in the blue box.  Since brands vary in size, we recommend weighing your graham crackers when measuring. 
  • Vegan butter. We like to use stick-style butter, melted, instead of dairy-free butter from a tub. We like the Country Crock plant butter sticks. 
  • Sugar and Salt. Both balance the crust’s flavor and sweetness level. 

For the cheesecake filling:

  • Vegan cream cheese. Softened to room temperature. Since this is the core of the recipe, choose your favorite brand. We like to use Violife, Tofutti, or the Philadelphia Dairy-free. 
  • Granulated sugar. To sweeten the filling. Opt for organic if you want to ensure it’s 100% vegan-friendly. 
  • Non-dairy milk. Make sure it’s room temperature so the coconut oil doesn’t start to solidify too soon. We like to use either soy milk or almond milk. 
  • Lemon juice. Helps add that signature cheesecake tang. Use fresh-squeezed lemon juice for best results. 
  • Vanilla extract. Enhances the flavor of both cheesecake layers. 
  • Salt. Balances the sweetness.
  • Coconut oil. To avoid an overly coconut-flavored filling, we recommend using refined coconut oil for this recipe. 
  • Pumpkin purée. Use pure pumpkin rather than pumpkin pie filling, which is already sweetened and spiced.
  • Pumpkin pie spice. A blend of your favorite fall spices. Think cinnamon, ginger, nutmeg, allspice, and clove.

Equipment Needed

  • Food processor
  • 8×8 inch baking dish
  • Parchment paper
  • Electric hand mixer or stand mixer
  • Large mixing bowl
  • Offset spatula
  • Kitchen scale
  • Rubber spatula

Tips for the Best Results

  • Cool the crust completely. Let it sit for at least 30 minutes. If you’re in a rush, you can also pop it in the fridge to cool. 
  • Bring your ingredients to room temperature. Keeping your cream cheese and milk at room temperature will prevent a lumpy filling or seizing the coconut oil. 
  • Cool the coconut oil. If you add melted coconut oil while it’s too hot, it can curdle the cream cheese, so make sure it has cooled to room temperature before mixing. 
  • Do not skip the freezer step between layers. A quick freeze firms up the vanilla layer just enough that the pumpkin layer sits on top instead of swirling into it.
  • Chill the full 8 hours. This helps the bars set properly. Slicing early gives you soft layers that will not hold their shape.

Serving Suggestions

These Pumpkin Cheesecake Bars are best served cold, directly from the fridge. You can also top them with a dollop of our 2-Ingredient Vegan Whipped Cream and a dusting of cinnamon, but they’re great on their own. Enjoy with a tall glass of soy or almond milk, coffee, tea, or chai latte. 

Storage Instructions

  • Refrigerator: Store the bars in an airtight container in the fridge for up to 5 days. Keep them cold, since the coconut oil softens at room temperature and causes the bars to lose their structure.
  • Freezer: These freeze beautifully. Slice them first, then freeze the bars in a single layer on a parchment-lined tray. Once solid, transfer to a freezer-safe container, with parchment between layers, and store for up to 2 months.
  • Thawing: Move frozen bars to the fridge for a few hours, or let them sit at room temperature for about 15 minutes. Some prefer them partially frozen, like ice cream bars, which is another great way to enjoy them. 

Recipe FAQs

Can I use a different pan size?

An 8×8 inch pan gives you nice thick bars. A 9×9 will work, but the layers will be thinner. We do not recommend a 9×13, since the layers become too thin to hold up.

My filling looks grainy or has little white flecks. What happened?

That is coconut oil seizing from contact with something cold. Usually, it’s cold milk or cold cream cheese. Everything should be at room temperature before mixing.

Can I substitute the coconut oil?

Not with a liquid oil, since the coconut oil is what makes the bars firm up in the fridge. Vegan butter can work as a substitute, though the set will be a little softer. Please note that we haven’t tested this option. 

Can I make this gluten-free?

Yes. Swap in gluten-free graham crackers or a similar crisp cookie and use the same 155g weight. Everything else in the recipe is already gluten-free.

Do I need a food processor for the crust?

It is the easiest way, but if you don’t have one, seal the graham crackers in a zip-top bag and crush them into crumbs with a rolling pin, then stir in the butter, sugar, and salt by hand.

Pumpkin Cheesecake Bars

5 Stars 4 Stars 3 Stars 2 Stars 1 Star

No reviews

  • Author: Chris Petrellese
  • Total Time: 8 hours 30 minutes
  • Yield: 9 Bars 1x
Print Recipe Pin Recipe

Description

These Pumpkin Cheesecake Bars are the perfect fall sweet treat. They’re layered with a golden graham cracker crust, a tangy dairy-free cheesecake, and a delicately spiced pumpkin cheesecake on top. This easy and fuss-free dessert comes together with minimal effort. The hardest part is just waiting for the bars to set. When it’s finished, you’ll have a beautifully layered treat to share with your friends and family. 

Ingredients

For the graham cracker crust

  • 10 (155g) vegan graham cracker sheets
  • 4 tablespoons vegan butter, melted
  • 1 teaspoon granulated sugar
  • Pinch of salt

For the cheesecake filling

  • 16 ounces vegan cream cheese, softened
  • 1/2 cup (100g) granulated sugar
  • 1/3 cup non-dairy milk, room temperature
  • 1 tablespoon lemon juice
  • 1/2 teaspoon vanilla extract
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1/3 cup plus 2 tablespoons coconut oil, melted and cooled, divided
  • 1/2 cup pumpkin purée
  • 1 teaspoon pumpkin pie spice

Instructions

Make the graham cracker crust

  1. Preheat the oven. Preheat the oven to 350ºF.
  2. Process the crumbs. In a food processor, process the graham cracker sheets until broken down into fine crumbs. Add the melted vegan butter, sugar, and salt, then pulse until combined. The mixture should resemble wet sand.
  3. Press into the pan. Line an 8×8 inch baking dish with parchment paper. Pour the graham cracker mixture into the dish and press evenly into the bottom.
  4. Bake and cool. Bake for 12 to 15 minutes, until the crust is golden brown and smells toasted. Remove from the oven and let cool for at least 30 minutes before continuing.

Make the cheesecake filling

  1. Whip the base. Add the softened vegan cream cheese and sugar to a large bowl. Using an electric hand mixer or stand mixer, beat until well combined and fluffy, about 2-3 minutes.
  2. Add the wet ingredients. Add the non-dairy milk, lemon juice, vanilla extract, salt, and 1/3 cup of the melted coconut oil. Mix until well combined and smooth.
  3. Check the pan temperature. Make sure the baking dish is not too hot, or the filling will melt. If needed, refrigerate it for a few minutes to cool.
  4. Add the first layer. Once the baking dish cools, spread half the filling over the graham cracker crust. Use an offset spatula to smooth it into an even layer.
  5. Set briefly. Place the baking dish in the freezer for about 10 minutes to firm up the cheesecake layer.
  6. Make the pumpkin layer. Add the remaining 2 tablespoons of coconut oil, the pumpkin purée, and the pumpkin pie spice to the remaining filling.
  7. Beat until smooth. Use your hand mixer to beat together until well combined and smooth.
  8. Add the second layer. Spoon the pumpkin filling over the vanilla cheesecake layer and smooth it into an even layer.
  9. Chill. Cover and refrigerate for at least 8 hours, or until set.
  10. Slice and serve. Once fully set, lift the bars out using the parchment overhang, slice, and enjoy.

Notes

  • Room temperature matters: Cold cream cheese or cold milk will cause the melted coconut oil to seize into flecks. Set everything out about an hour ahead.
  • Cool the crust fully: A warm pan will melt the coconut oil out of the filling, and the bars will not set properly.
  • Clean slices: Wipe your knife between cuts with a warm, damp cloth for the sharpest edges.
  • Storage: Refrigerate for up to 5 days, or freeze sliced bars for up to 2 months.
  • Prep Time: 8 Hours 15 Minutes
  • Cook Time: 15 Minutes

Did you make this recipe?

Where Chef Eric Sze Eats on His Day Off in NYC

📅 Oct 19, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Chef Eric Sze, the owner of NYC’s Wenwen, shows us the bakeries, pizzerias, and restaurants he visits weekly for everything from simple baguette sandwiches to oversized wontons.

Sze starts his day off with a sandwich at Bakeri; his go-to order is honey, apple, and brie layered on a crunchy baguette. Then he heads to The Meat Hook, Wenwen’s meat supplier, to get a specialty cut of steak and dry-aged short ribs for dinner. Next, he grabs a whole, crispy, NY-style pie at Chrissy’s Pizza (chef Chris Hansell has been perfecting this pie for years). The jalapeño and garlic pie has perfect proportions of each ingredient, all on a doughy, yeasted crust.

For his final stop, Sze goes to Maxi’s Noodle 2 in Flushing for the nostalgic Chinese food he’s craved since leaving Taiwan. Owner Maxi Lau-O’Keefe teaches him how to make her iconic wontons, filled with shrimp and pork before being expertly folded. After being humbled by the complex folding process, Sze settles in for a soup full of thin noodles made with duck eggs and those classic wontons, alongside beef soup.

Watch the most recent episode of Chef’s Day Off to see how chef Eric Sze spends his day off at his favorite restaurants, bakeries, and butcher shops around New York City.

The humble potato has long been a reliable go-to side dish, showing up on our plates in familiar forms like mashed, boiled, roasted, and fried. But potatoes can be so much more. When I pile them high with flavorful toppings, hearty fillings, melty cheese, and plenty of delicious extras, they go from a simple side to a seriously craveable dish. Whether you’re looking for an easy weeknight dinner, an appetizer to bring to a party, or the ultimate comfort food, these loaded potato recipes prove just how versatile this humble vegetable can be.

Take Your Potatoes From Side Dish to Showstopper!

These loaded potato recipes take our beloved spud to the next level: crispy potato skins overflowing with cheese and bacon, fluffy baked potatoes piled high with savory toppings, and loads of appetizing combinations that turn this everyday ingredient into something indulgent. Whether you’re looking for an easy weeknight side, a satisfying snack, or a dish that’s sure to disappear at a party, these recipes prove that there’s no such thing as too many toppings when it comes to potatoes. Here are our favorite loaded potato recipes to get you started!

Tips for Beginners

  • Use fresh potatoes. You may be able to buy potato skins or prepared mashed potatoes at the grocery store, but nothing beats freshly made.
  • Don’t waste any part of the potato. If you’re using the skins, save the insides, and if you are using the insides, save the skins.
  • A quick broil crisps things up. If you find the cheese hasn’t browned or crisped up, put the dish under the broiler for just a few minutes.
  • Get creative. I use my favorite toppings like chives and bacon, but the sky’s the limit. Try sprinkling olives, capers, and chopped veggies; swap the cheese; or use plant-based cheese.
